\theta|\Gamma^{\prime}|
points of
\Gamma^{\prime}
,
|V_{1}|\geq\theta|\Gamma^{\prime}|\geq\theta|V|
, and therefore
|V_{0}|\leq\theta^{-1}
. By averaging, we can find
v\in V_{0}
such that
|\Gamma^{\prime}\cap(v+V_{1})|\geq\theta^{2}|\Gamma^{\prime}|
.

Now
v+V_{1}
is the graph of an affine map from
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}
to
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}
, so this is implies that there is a subset
B^{\prime}
of
B
of size at least
\theta^{2}\alpha^{16/3}|G|/6
such that the restriction of
\sigma
to
B^{\prime}
is affine.

From this, we obtain the following lemma (which is also a lemma of
[
4
]
, but there it is proved for
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
and with a significantly worse bound, because the results of Sanders were not yet known).

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
\gamma,\epsilon>0
, let
G=\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}
, let
f:G^{2}\to\mathbb{C}
be a bounded function, let
g=f\mathop{\multimapdotbothvert}f
, and let
\Sigma
be the set of all pairs
(h,u)
such that
|\widehat{g_{\bullet h}}(u)|^{2}\geq\gamma
. Then there are affine functions
T_{1},\dots,T_{m}
such that for all but at most
\epsilon|G|
points
(h,u)
of
\Sigma
there exists
i
with
T_{i}h=u
. Moreover, we may take
m
to be
\exp(2^{56}(\log(\epsilon^{-1})+\log(\gamma^{-1})+\log p)^{6})
. In particular,
m
has a quasipolynomial dependence on
\gamma
and
\epsilon
.

Proof.

The idea of the proof is simple: we use the results just discussed to remove from
\Sigma
large pieces that are restrictions of graphs of affine functions until we have removed almost everything.

The inductive step is as follows. Suppose we have a subset
\Sigma^{\prime}\subset\Sigma
of size at least
\epsilon|G|
. Since for each
h
there are at most
\gamma^{-1}
values of
u
with
(h,u)\in\Sigma
(by Parseval), we can find a set
B
of size at least
\gamma\epsilon|G|
and a function
\sigma:\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}\to(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n})^{*}
such that
\mathbb{E}_{h}\mathbbm{1}_{B}(h)|\widehat{g_{\bullet h}}(\sigma(h))|^{2}\geq\gamma^{2}\epsilon
. Setting
\alpha=\gamma^{2}\epsilon
, we may now apply the result from the discussion above to obtain a subset
B^{\prime}
of size at least
\theta^{2}\alpha^{16/3}|G|/6
, where
\theta=\exp(-2^{48}(\log(\alpha^{-1})+\log p)^{6})
, such that the restriction of
\sigma
to
B^{\prime}
is affine.

Since
|\Sigma|\leq\gamma^{-1}|G|
(again by Parseval), we cannot repeat this process more than
6\theta^{-2}\alpha^{-16/3}\gamma^{-1}=6\theta^{-2}\epsilon^{-16/3}\gamma^{-35/3}
times, and when it stops we are left with a set of size at most
\epsilon|G|
. We may therefore take
m
to be

6\exp(2^{49}(\log(\epsilon^{-1})+2\log(\gamma^{-1})+\log p)^{6})\epsilon^{-16/3}\gamma^{-35/3}\leq\exp(2^{56}(\log(\epsilon^{-1})+\log(\gamma^{-1})+\log p)^{6}).

This proves the lemma.
∎

4.7.
L_{2}
approximation in the bilinear case: the main result

We now have the ingredients we need to prove that a mixed convolution
F
of a bounded function on
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}\times\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}
can be approximated in
L_{2}
by
T_{\beta}F
, where
T_{\beta}
is the averaging projection on to the level sets of a bilinear map
\beta:\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}\times\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
for some not too large
k
.

To recap, we begin with a bounded function
f:\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}\times\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
and we let
F=\mathop{\talloblong\!}f
. Lemma
4.7
implies that
\|\widehat{F_{x\bullet}}\|_{1}\leq 1
for every
x
.

Let
\epsilon,\gamma>0
be constants to be chosen later. Letting
g=f\mathop{\multimapdotbothvert}f
(so that
F=g\mathop{\multimapdotboth}g
), we also have from Lemma
4.10
some affine functions
T_{1},\dots,T_{m}:\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}
such that for all but at most
\epsilon|G|
values of
h
, the
\gamma
-large spectrum of
g_{\bullet h}
(meaning here the set of
u
such that
|\widehat{g_{\bullet h}}(u)|^{2}\geq\gamma
) is contained in the set
\{T_{1}h,\dots,T_{m}h\}
. Moreover,
m
has the quasipolynomial dependence on
\epsilon
and
\gamma
given in Lemma
4.10
.

Next, let us define for each
i
a function
u_{i}:\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
by setting

u_{i}(y)=\begin{cases}0&T_{j}y=T_{i}y\ \mbox{for some }j<i\\
\widehat{F_{\bullet y}}(T_{i}y)&\mbox{otherwise}\\
\end{cases}

Then define a function
G
by the formula

G(x,y)=G_{\bullet y}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}u_{i}(y)\omega^{x.T_{i}y}=\sum_{v\in\{T_{1}y,\dots,T_{m}y\}}\widehat{F_{\bullet y}}(v)\omega^{x.v}.

Then for each
y
,
\widehat{G_{\bullet y}}
is the restriction of
\widehat{F_{\bullet y}}
to the set
\{T_{1}y,\dots,T_{m}y\}
. If
y
is such that the
\gamma
-large spectrum of
g_{\bullet y}
is contained in this set, then

\|\widehat{G_{\bullet y}}-\widehat{F_{\bullet y}}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\|\widehat{G_{\bullet y}}-\widehat{F_{\bullet y}}\|_{1}\|\widehat{G_{\bullet y}}-\widehat{F_{\bullet y}}\|_{\infty}\leq\gamma,

where the second inequality follows from the fact that
\hat{F}_{\bullet y}=|\hat{g}_{\bullet y}|^{2}
. Also, for every
y
we have that
\|G_{\bullet y}-F_{\bullet y}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 1
. Since the first inequality holds for all but at most
\epsilon|G|
values of
y
, it follows that
\|G-F\|_{2}^{2}\leq\gamma+\epsilon
.

Define
v_{i}(y)
to be
\mathbb{E}_{x}F(x,y)\omega^{-x.T_{i}y}
. Writing
S_{i}(y)
for the set
\{j:T_{j}y=T_{i}y\}
, we have
u_{i}=v_{i}.\mathbbm{1}_{\{y:i=\min S_{i}(y)\}}
.

The rough idea now is that the functions
v_{i}
are “nice”, and each
u_{i}
is built out of a few restrictions of
v_{i}
to subspaces, which makes the
u_{i}
“nice” too (but less so). The details are as follows.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
v_{i}
be defined as above. Then
\|\widehat{v_{i}}\|_{1}\leq 1
.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that
\|\widehat{F_{x\bullet}}\|_{1}\leq 1
for every
x
. Indeed,

\displaystyle\widehat{v_{i}}(w)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x,y}F(x,y)\omega^{-x.T_{i}y-w.y}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}\widehat{F_{x\bullet}}(T_{i}^{*}x+w).

It follows that

\|\widehat{v_{i}}\|_{1}\leq\mathbb{E}_{x}\sum_{w}|\widehat{F_{x\bullet}}(T_{i}^{*}x+w)|=\mathbb{E}_{x}\|\widehat{F_{x\bullet}}\|_{1}\leq 1,

as claimed.
∎

That is the sense in which the
v_{i}
are nice. Now we turn to the
u_{i}
. To begin with, we recall a simple fact about Fourier transforms of subspaces.

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
V
be a subspace of
G=\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}
. Then
\|\widehat{\mathbbm{1}_{V}}\|_{1}=1
.

Proof.

This is a direct calculation.

\mathbbm{1}_{V}(u)=\mathbb{E}_{x}\mathbbm{1}_{V}(x)\omega^{-x.u}=\begin{cases}|V|/|G|&u\in V^{\perp}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}\\
\end{cases}.

Since
|V||V^{\perp}|=|G|
, the result follows.
∎

Corollary 4.13
.

Let
V
be a subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}
and let
f:\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
. Then
\|\widehat{f\mathbbm{1}_{V}}\|_{1}\leq\|\hat{f}\|_{1}
.

Proof.

The result follows from Lemma
4.12
and Lemma
4.6
, since

\widehat{f\mathbbm{1}_{V}}=\hat{f}\bar{*}\widehat{\mathbbm{1}_{V}}
.
∎

Now observe that for each
i
the characteristic function of the set
\{y:i=\min S_{i}(y)\}
can be written as a
\pm 1
combination of at most
2^{m}
characteristic functions of subspaces, by the inclusion-exclusion formula. Indeed,

\mathbbm{1}_{\{y:i=\min S_{i}(y)\}}=\prod_{j<i}(1-\mathbbm{1}_{\{y:j\in S_{i}\}}).

The right-hand side is a
\pm 1
combination of characteristic functions of intersections of subspaces of the form
\{y:T_{j}y=T_{i}y\}
, and there are at most
2^{m}
such intersections.

Therefore, the
\ell_{1}
-norm of the Fourier transform of
\mathbbm{1}_{\{y:i=\min S_{i}(y)\}}
is at most
2^{m}
. By Lemmas
4.11
and
4.13
and the fact that
u_{i}=v_{i}.\mathbbm{1}_{y:i=\min S_{i}(y)}
, it follows that
\|\widehat{u_{i}}\|_{1}\leq 2^{m}
.

Let us summarize what we have proved in the form of a lemma.

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
f:\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}\times\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
be a bounded function and let
F
be the mixed convolution
\mathop{\talloblong\!}f
. Then for every
\delta>0
there exist linear maps
T_{1},\dots,T_{m}:\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}
and functions
u_{1},\dots,u_{m}:\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
such that
\|\widehat{u_{i}}\|_{1}\leq 2^{m}
for each
i
and such that setting
G(x,y)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}u_{i}(y)\omega^{x.T_{i}y}
we have that
\|G-F\|_{2}^{2}\leq\delta
. Moreover,
m
can be taken to equal
\exp(2^{63}(\log(\delta^{-1})+\log p)^{6})
.

Proof.

Set
\epsilon=\gamma=\delta/2
in the discussion above. Then, as we showed,
\|G-F\|_{2}^{2}\leq\epsilon+\gamma=\delta
. The bound for
m
comes from setting
\epsilon=\gamma=\delta/2
in the bound in Lemma
4.10
.
∎

We are ready for one of the main results of this section, which may be of independent interest.

Theorem 4.15
.

For every
\zeta>0
there exists a positive integer
k
with the following property. Let
G=\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}
and let
f:G\times G\to\mathbb{C}
be any bounded function. Then there is a bi-affine map
\beta:G^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
such that, writing
F
for the mixed convolution
\mathop{\talloblong\!}f
and
P_{\beta}
for the averaging projection on to the level sets of
\beta
, we have the approximation
\|F-P_{\beta}F\|_{2}\leq\zeta
. Moreover,
k
can be taken to be
4m^{3}2^{m}/\zeta^{2}
, where
m=\exp(2^{69}(\log(\zeta^{-1})+\log p)^{6})
.

Proof.

We do a further approximation by truncating the Fourier transforms of the
u_{i}
. Let
\delta=\zeta/2
. For each
i
,
\|\hat{u}_{i}\|_{1}\leq 2^{m}
, so we can find a set
K_{i}
of size at most
m^{2}2^{m}/\delta^{2}
such that
|\widehat{u_{i}}(v)|<\delta^{2}/m^{2}
for every
v\notin K_{i}
. Let
w_{i}(y)=\sum_{v\in K_{i}}\widehat{u_{i}}(v)\omega^{v.y}
. Then

\|u_{i}-w_{i}\|_{2}^{2}=\|\widehat{u_{i}}-\widehat{w_{i}}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\|\widehat{u_{i}}-\widehat{w_{i}}\|_{1}\|\widehat{u_{i}}-\widehat{w_{i}}\|_{\infty}\leq\delta^{2}/m^{2}

for each
i
. It follows that the function
y\mapsto(u_{i}(y)-w_{i}(y))\omega^{x.T_{i}y}
has
L_{2}
norm at most
\delta/m
, so if we set
H(x,y)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}w_{i}(y)\omega^{x.T_{i}y}
, then
\|H-G\|_{2}\leq\delta
, by the triangle inequality, and therefore
\|H-F\|_{2}\leq 2\delta
, also by the triangle inequality.

Let us write
K_{i}=\{v_{i1},\dots,v_{ik_{i}}\}
. Then

H(x,y)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{i}}\widehat{u_{i}}(v_{ij})\omega^{(T_{i}^{*}x+v_{ij}).y}.

Writing
\lambda_{ij}
for
\widehat{u_{i}}(v_{ij})
and
\beta_{ij}(x,y)
for
(T_{i}^{*}x+v_{ij}).y
, we can rewrite this as
\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{i}}\lambda_{ij}\omega^{\beta_{ij}(x,y)}
. That is, we have approximated
F
in
L_{2}
by a linear combination of a bounded number of bilinear phase functions.

Now define a bilinear map
\beta:\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
, where
k=\sum_{i=1}^{m}k_{i}
by concatenating all the
\beta_{ij}
. That is, we set

\beta(x,y)=(\beta_{11}(x,y),\dots,\beta_{1k_{1}}(x,y),\dots,\beta_{m1}(x,y),\dots,\beta_{mk_{m}}(x,y)).

Then
H
is constant on the level sets of
\beta
. But the closest approximation to
F
in
L_{2}
by a function that is constant on these level sets is
P_{\beta}F
, where
P_{\beta}
is the averaging projection. It follows that
\|F-P_{\beta}F\|_{2}\leq 2\delta=\zeta
.

Since each
K_{i}
has size at most
m^{2}2^{m}/\delta^{2}
, we have the bound
k\leq m^{3}2^{m}/\delta^{2}=4m^{3}2^{m}/\zeta^{2}
. The bound now follows from substituting
\zeta/2
for
\delta
in the bound for
m
in Lemma
4.14
.
∎

The dependence of
k
on
\zeta
is given by a polylogarithmic function followed by an exponential one. The exponential part of this comes from our use of the inclusion-exclusion argument just after Corollary
4.13
. It is not obvious that it is needed, so it is conceivable that an exponential can be saved in this part of the argument.

In a forthcoming paper
[
6
]
we shall prove a theorem that describes a

4.8.
Obtaining a near bihomomorphism with respect to a linear combination of the level sets of a bilinear function

We have done two things in this section so far: we have obtained a near bihomomorphism
\psi
with respect to a measure of the form
F=\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mathbbm{1}_{A}
, and we have shown that the mixed convolution
\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mathbbm{1}_{A}
can be approximated in
L_{2}
by
P_{\beta}F
, where
P_{\beta}
is the averaging projection to the level sets of a bilinear function
\beta:\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}\times\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
, with
k
depending only on the density of
A
. We complete this section by checking that if the
L_{2}
approximation is good enough, then
\psi
is a near bihomomorphism on
P_{\beta}F
as well.

First we give a standard argument to show that a reasonably dense measure contains many 4-arrangements.

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
G
be a finite Abelian group and let
\mu
be a non-negative function defined on
G^{2}
. Then
\bigl\|\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mu\bigr\|_{2}^{2}\geq\|\mu\|_{1}^{8}
.

Proof.

First, note that if
F
is any non-negative function defined on
G^{2}
, then

\displaystyle\|F\mathop{\multimapdotboth}F\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{y}\|F_{\bullet y}\bar{*}F_{\bullet y}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{y}\|F_{\bullet y}\|_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq(\mathbb{E}_{y}\|F_{\bullet y}\|_{1})^{2}

\displaystyle=\|F\|_{1}^{2}

and that the same proof gives the corresponding inequality for vertical convolutions as well.

We therefore have

\displaystyle\bigl\|\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mu\,\bigr\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\bigl\|\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mu\,\bigr\|_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|(\mu\mathop{\multimapdotbothvert}\mu)\mathop{\multimapdotboth}(\mu\mathop{\multimapdotbothvert}\mu)\|_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\|\mu\mathop{\multimapdotbothvert}\mu\|_{1}^{4}

\displaystyle\geq\|\mu\|_{1}^{8},

which proves the lemma.
∎

Lemma 4.17
.

Let
G
be a finite Abelian group and let
f_{1},\dots,f_{4}
be bounded functions from
G^{2}
to the group algebra
\mathcal{A}
of
G
. Then
\|\mathop{\talloblong\!}(f_{1},\dots,f_{4})\|_{2}\leq\max_{i}\|f_{i}\|_{2}
.

Proof.

Recall that if
f
and
g
are two functions from
G
to
\mathcal{A}
, then we define the convolution
f\bar{*}g
by

f\bar{*}g(x)=\mathbb{E}_{u-v=x}f(u)g(v)^{*}
, which then allows us to define horizontal and vertical convolutions in the same way as for scalar-valued functions.

Now note that if
F
and
F^{\prime}
are bounded functions on
G^{2}
, then

\displaystyle\|F\mathop{\multimapdotboth}F^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{y}\|F_{\bullet y}\bar{*}F^{\prime}_{\bullet y}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{y}\|F_{\bullet y}\bar{*}F^{\prime}_{\bullet y}\|_{\infty}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{y}\|F_{\bullet y}\|_{2}^{2}\|F^{\prime}_{\bullet y}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{y}\|F_{\bullet y}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|F\|_{2}^{2}.

The same proof also gives the corresponding inequality for vertical convolutions. Therefore, since all the relevant functions are bounded,

\displaystyle\|\mathop{\talloblong\!}(f_{1},\dots,f_{4})\,\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\|(f_{1}\mathop{\multimapdotbothvert}f_{2})\mathop{\multimapdotboth}(f_{3}\mathop{\multimapdotbothvert}f_{4})\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|f_{1}\mathop{\multimapdotbothvert}f_{2}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|f_{1}\|_{2}.

By symmetry, the inequality holds for the other
f_{i}
, and the lemma is proved.
∎

Now let
\mu
and
\nu
be non-negative bounded functions on
G^{2}
and let
\psi
be a
(1-\eta)
-bihomomorphism with respect to
\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mu
. Suppose also that
\psi:G^{2}\to\mathcal{A}
is a
(1-\eta)
-bihomomorphism with respect to
\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mu
. That is,

\mathbb{E}_{w,h}\bigl\|\mathbb{E}_{P\in\mathcal{P}(w,h)}(\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mu)(P)\psi(P)\bigr\|_{2}^{2}\geq(1-\eta)\mathbb{E}_{w,h}\bigl|(\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mu)(w,h)\bigr|^{2}.

Note that the expression
\mathbb{E}_{P\in\mathcal{P}(w,h)}(\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mu)(P)\psi(P)
is equal to
\mathop{\talloblong\!}(\mu\phi)(w,h)
, so a more concise formulation of the above inequality is

\bigl\|\,\mathop{\talloblong\!}(\mu\psi)\bigr\|_{2}^{2}\geq(1-\eta)\bigl\|\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mu\bigr\|_{2}^{2}

(where the
L_{2}
norm on the left-hand side is a sum of squares of
L_{2}
-norms of elements of
\mathcal{A}
).

We would like to deduce a similar inequality for
\nu
when
\|\mu-\nu\|_{2}
is sufficiently small. To do this, note first that
\bigl\|\,\mathop{\talloblong\!}(\mu\psi)\bigr\|_{2}^{2}=\langle\mathop{\talloblong\!}(\mu\psi),\mathop{\talloblong\!}(\mu\psi)\rangle
and similarly for
\nu
, so
\bigl\|\,\mathop{\talloblong\!}(\mu\psi)\bigr\|_{2}^{2}-\bigl\|\,\mathop{\talloblong\!}(\nu\psi)\bigr\|_{2}^{2}
is equal to a sum of eight terms of the form

\langle\mathop{\talloblong\!}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{4}),\mathop{\talloblong\!}(\xi_{5},\xi_{6},\xi_{7},\xi_{8})\rangle,

where for the
i
th such term we have
\xi_{1}=\dots=\xi_{i-1}=\nu\psi
,
\xi_{i}=(\mu-\nu)\psi
, and
\xi_{i+1}=\dots=\xi_{8}=\mu\psi
.
Both sides of the inner product are bounded functions, and hence have
L_{2}
norm at most 1. But by Lemma
4.17
one of the sides of each term has
L_{2}
-norm at most
\|\mu-\nu\|_{2}
, so by Cauchy-Schwarz each term is at most
\|\mu-\nu\|_{2}
and therefore the sum of the eight terms is at most
8\|\mu-\nu\|_{2}
.

If we take
\psi
to be the function taking the constant value
\delta_{0}
in the above argument, we obtain also the inequality
\Bigl|\bigl\|\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mu\bigr\|_{2}^{2}-\bigl\|\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mu\bigr\|_{2}^{2}\Bigr|\leq 8\|\mu-\nu\|_{2}
as well.

This gives us the following lemma.

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
G
be a finite Abelian group with group algebra
\mathcal{A}
, let
\mu
and
\nu
be non-negative bounded functions on
G^{2}
and suppose that
\|\mu-\nu\|_{2}\leq(\eta/16)\|\mu\|_{1}^{8}
. Let
\psi:G^{2}\to\mathcal{A}
be a
(1-\eta)
-bihomomorphism with respect to
\mu
. Then
\psi
is a
(1-2\eta)
-bihomomorphism with respect to
\nu
.

Proof.

By hypothesis, we have the inequality

\bigl\|\,\mathop{\talloblong\!}(\mu\psi)\bigr\|_{2}^{2}\geq(1-\eta)\bigl\|\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mu\bigr\|_{2}^{2}.

If we replace
\mu
by
\nu
, then the calculations above show that neither side of this inequality changes by more than
8\|\mu-\nu\|_{2}
. By asumption, this is at most
(\eta/2)\|\mu_{1}\|^{8}
, which in turn is at most
(\eta/2)\bigl\|\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mu\bigr\|_{2}^{2}
, by Lemma
4.16
. Therefore,

\bigl\|\,\mathop{\talloblong\!}(\nu\psi)\bigr\|_{2}^{2}\geq(1-\eta/2)\bigl\|\,\mathop{\talloblong\!}(\mu\psi)\bigr\|_{2}^{2}\geq(1-3\eta/2)\bigl\|\,\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mu\bigr\|_{2}^{2}\geq(1-3\eta/2)(1+\eta/2)^{-1}\bigl\|\mathop{\talloblong\!}\nu\bigr\|_{2}^{2},

which gives us our conclusion, since
(1-3\eta/2)(1+\eta/2)^{-1}\geq 1-2\eta
.
∎

Now let us draw everything together and give the second main result of this section.

Theorem 4.19
.

For every
\alpha,\eta,\zeta>0
such that
\zeta\leq\alpha^{4}\eta/16
there exists
k
with the following property. Let
G=\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}
, let
\mathcal{A}
be its group algebra, let
A\subset G
be a set of density
\alpha
, and let
\phi:G^{2}\to\mathcal{A}
be a second-order
(1-\eta)
-bihomomorphism with respect to
\mathbbm{1}_{A}
. Let
\mu=\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mathbbm{1}_{A}
and let
\psi:G^{2}\to\Sigma(\mathcal{A})
be defined by the equation
(\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mu)\psi=\mathop{\talloblong\!}(\mathbbm{1}_{A}\phi)
(with
\psi
taking an arbitrary value at
(w,h)
if
\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mu(w,h)=0
). Then there is a bi-affine map
\beta:G^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
and a non-negative function
\nu:G^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\nu(x,y)
depends only on
\beta(x,y)
,
\psi
is a
(1-2\eta)
-bihomomorphism with respect to
\nu
, and
\|\mu-\nu\|_{2}\leq\zeta
. Moreover, we may take
k
to be
2^{10}m^{3}2^{m}/\alpha^{4}\eta
, where
m=\exp(2^{69}(\log(\zeta^{-1})+\log p)^{6})
.

Proof.

Lemma
4.1
(with the definition of
\psi
in its proof) tells us that
\psi
is a
(1-\eta)
-bihomomorphism with respect to
\mu=\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mathbbm{1}_{A}
. The proof of Lemma
4.16
gives us that
\|\mu\|_{1}\geq\|\mathbbm{1}_{A}\|_{1}^{4}=\alpha^{4}
. Now we apply Theorem
4.15
to obtain a positive integer
k
and a bilinear map
\beta:G^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
such that, writing
P_{\beta}
for the averaging projection to the level sets of
\beta
, we have that
\|\mu-P_{\beta}\mu\|_{2}\leq\zeta
. Setting
\nu=P_{\beta}\mu
, we have that
\nu
is constant on the level sets of
\beta
and, by Lemma
4.18
and the bound on
\zeta
, that
\psi
is a
(1-2\eta)
-bihomomorphism with respect to
\nu
. The bound for
k
comes from Theorem
4.15
.
∎

5.
Obtaining a near bihomomorphism on a high-rank bilinear Bohr set

Our rough aim now is to deduce from the conclusion of Theorem
4.19
that
\psi
is a near bihomomorphism on one of the level sets of
\beta
, and then, using structural information about these level sets, to prove a stability result that allows us to approximate
\psi
by the restriction of an exact bihomomorphism defined on all of
(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n})^{2}
. However, before we do this, we shall need a few results about bilinear maps. In particular, as several other authors have found, there are many arguments that work only for bilinear maps of sufficiently large rank. Ours are no exception, so we shall need a lemma that tells us that we can obtain this condition if we do not have it already.

5.1.
The rank of a bilinear map

Let
\beta:\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}\times\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
be a bilinear form. We can write it in the form
\beta(x,y)=x.Ty
for some linear map
T:\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}
: the
rank
of
\beta
is defined to be the rank of
T
. If
\beta
has rank
t
, then
\mathbb{E}_{x,y}\omega^{\beta(x,y)}=p^{-t}
, since if
Ty=0
, then the expectation over
x
is 1, and otherwise it is 0, and the probability that
Ty=0
is
p^{-t}
. Thus, we can define the rank of
\beta
in an equivalent analytic way as
-\log_{p}(\mathbb{E}_{x,y}\omega^{\beta(x,y)})
.

If
\beta
is bi-affine rather than bilinear, meaning that it has a formula of the form

\beta(x,y)=x.Ty+x.u+v.y+\lambda,

where
T
is a linear map as before,
u,v\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}
, and
\lambda\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
, then we again define the rank of
\beta
to be the rank of
T
. The analytic characterization no longer works, but it does if we modify it in an obvious way. If we define
f(x,y)
to be
\omega^{\beta(x,y)}
, then

f(x,y)\overline{f(x-a,y)f(x,y-b)}f(x-a,y-b)=\omega^{a.Tb}

for every
x,y
, so a suitable definition is

-\log_{p}\bigl(\mathbb{E}_{x,y,a,b}f(x,y)\overline{f(x-a,y)f(x,y-b)}f(x-a,y-b)\bigr)=-\log_{p}\|f\|_{\square}^{4},

where
\|.\|_{\square}
is the box norm for functions defined on
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}\times\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}
. As we shall see later, this analytic definition of rank is worth making because it can be generalized straightforwardly and usefully to multilinear maps.

The box norm is a well-known measure of quasirandomness. Thus, if we have a bilinear form of high rank, it enables us to use quasirandomness properties in our proofs.

We are concerned not just with bilinear forms but with bilinear maps to
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
for small
k
. It would be natural to call
k
the rank (or at least an upper bound for the rank) of such a map. But since that word is taken, we shall call
k
the
dimension
of a bilinear map
\beta:\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}\times\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
. By a
bilinear set
, we shall mean a set of the form
B_{\beta}=\{(x,y):\beta(x,y)=0\}
, where
\beta
is such a map. We shall call
k
the
codimension
of
B_{\beta}
. We shall use the same terminology for bi-affine sets, which are defined as above but with
\beta
a bi-affine map instead. Rather than continually mentioning bi-affine maps, we shall talk about bilinear maps on the understanding that what we say applies to bi-affine maps as well (unless we make clear that this is not the case).

We define the
rank
of a bilinear map
\beta:\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}\times\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
to be the smallest rank of any bilinear form
(x,y)\mapsto u.\beta(x,y)
such that
u
is a non-zero element of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
. That is, if
\beta=(\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{k})
, it is the smallest rank of a non-trivial linear combination of the bilinear forms
\beta_{i}
.

Let
X
and
Y
be vector spaces over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
and let
X^{\prime}\subset X
and
Y^{\prime}\subset Y
be affine subspaces. Define a
bilinear Bohr set of codimension

k
in
X^{\prime}\times Y^{\prime}
to be a set of the form
\{(x,y)\in X^{\prime}\times Y^{\prime}:\beta(x,y)=z\}
, where
\beta
is a bi-affine map from
X^{\prime}\times Y^{\prime}
to some other space
Z
. In other words, it is one of the “level sets” we talked about earlier. The
rank
of a bilinear Bohr set is the highest possible rank of a bi-affine map of which it is a level set. (The reason the definition is phrased like that is that, as easy but artificial examples show, two bi-affine maps of different ranks can have the same level sets: for instance, just take the direct sum of a bilinear map with the zero map.)

Let
X,Y
and
Z
be vector spaces over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
and let
\beta:X\times Y\to Z
be a bilinear map. Suppose we have direct-sum decompositions
X=X_{0}+X_{1}
and
Y=Y_{0}+Y_{1}
. If we write a typical element
(x,y)
of
X\times Y
as
(x_{0}+x_{1},y_{0}+y_{1})
in the obvious way, then we have that

\beta(x,y)=\beta_{00}(x_{0},y_{0})+\beta_{01}(x_{0},y_{1})+\beta_{10}(x_{1},y_{0})+\beta_{11}(x_{1},y_{1}),

where
\beta_{ij}
is the restriction of
\beta
to
X_{i}\times Y_{j}
. For each
(v,w,z)\in X_{0}\times Y_{0}\times Z
, let
B_{v,w,z}
be the set of all
(x_{0}+x_{1},y_{0}+y_{1})\in X\times Y
such that
x_{0}=v
,
y_{0}=w
, and
\beta(x,y)=z
. This is a bilinear Bohr set inside the affine subspace
\{(x,y):x_{0}=v,y_{0}=w\}
. We call the decomposition of
X\times Y
into the bilinear Bohr sets
B_{v,w,z}
a
bilinear Bohr decomposition
. Its
rank
is the smallest rank of any of the sets
B_{v,w,z}
(with the convention that the rank of the empty set is infinite, as is the rank of a product of two affine subspaces).

The basic idea behind Corollary
5.2
below is reasonably standard, even if the details are less so. Given a bi-affine map
\beta:X\times Y\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
, where
X
and
Y
are copies of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}
, our aim is to find a bilinear Bohr decomposition of
X\times Y
of high rank, using
\beta
, with the dimensions of
X_{0}
and
Y_{0}
not too large.

We make one more set of definitions before starting on our argument. Given vector spaces
X,Y
and
Z
over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, every bi-affine map
\beta:X\times Y\to Z
, it has a formula of the form

\beta(x,y)=\gamma(x,y)+Ax+By+z

where
\gamma
is bilinear,
A:X\to Z
and
B:Y\to Z
are linear and
z\in Z
. We can say explicitly what these various components are. Indeed, they are given by the following formulae.

(1)

\gamma(x,y)=\beta(x,y)-\beta(x,0)-\beta(0,y)+\beta(0,0)
.

(2)

Ax=\beta(x,0)-\beta(0,0)
.

(3)

By=\beta(0,y)-\beta(0,0)
.

(4)

z=\beta(0,0)
.

We shall call
\gamma
the
bilinear part
of
\beta
. We shall say that two bi-affine maps are
equivalent
if they have the same bilinear part, and we shall say that a bi-affine map is
linear
if its bilinear part is zero. We have already commented that two equivalent bi-affine maps have the same rank.

The next result will serve as an inductive step.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
V,W
and
X
be vector spaces over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
and let
\beta:V\times W\to X
be a bi-affine map of rank at most
t
. Then there are subspaces
V^{\prime}\subset V
and
W^{\prime}\subset W
of codimension at most
t
and a non-zero vector
u\in X
such that
u.\beta
is linear on every product
(V^{\prime}+v)\times(W^{\prime}+w)
of translates of
V^{\prime}
and
W^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By hypothesis, there is some non-zero
u\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
such that the bi-affine form
(x,y)\mapsto u.\beta(x,y)
has rank at most
t
. Let
u.\beta(x,y)
be given by the formula
x.Ty+x.a+b.y+\lambda
, where
T
is a linear map of rank at most
t
. Let
V^{\prime}=\ker T^{*}
and let
W^{\prime}=\ker T
. Then
x.Ty=0
for every
(x,y)\in V^{\prime}\times W^{\prime}
. (We also have that
x.Ty=0
for every
(x,y)\in V^{\prime}\times W
, but we prefer a more symmetrical conclusion.) It follows that for all such
(x,y)
and for every
(v,w)
,

u.\beta(x+v,y+w)=x.(Tw+a)+(T^{*}v+b).y+u.\beta(v,w),

and therefore that
\beta
is linear on each product
(V^{\prime}+v)\times(W^{\prime}+w)
. Since the spaces
V^{\prime}
and
W^{\prime}
have codimension at most
t
, we are done.
∎

A simple fact that will be useful in the next proof is that the rank of the restriction of a biaffine map
\beta
to a product of translates
(V^{\prime}+v)\times(W^{\prime}+w)
does not depend on
v
and
w
. Indeed, if the restriction of
u.\beta
to
V^{\prime}\times W^{\prime}
is given by the formula
u.\beta(x,y)=x.Ty+x.a+b.y+\lambda
, then, as we essentially saw at the end of the proof above, the restriction to
(V^{\prime}+v)\times(W^{\prime}+w)
is given by the formula
u.\beta(x+v,y+w)=x.Ty+x.(Tw+a)+(T^{*}v+b).y+u.\beta(v,w)
, so it is an equivalent bi-affine map to the restriction to
V^{\prime}\times W^{\prime}
.

Corollary 5.2
.

Let
X,Y
and
Z
be vector spaces over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, let
\beta:X\times Y\to Z
be a bilinear map, and let
t
be a positive integer. Then there are decompositions
X=X_{0}+X_{1}
and
Y=Y_{0}+Y_{1}
such that
X_{0}
and
Y_{0}
have dimension at most
t\dim Z
and the resulting Bohr decomposition has rank at least
t
.

Proof.

Let
V_{0}=X
,
W_{0}=Y
and
\beta_{0}=\beta
. If
\beta
is of rank at least
t
, then we can take the decompositions
\{0\}+V_{0}
and
\{0\}+W_{0}
. Otherwise, by Lemma
5.1
there exist
u\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
and subspaces
V_{1}\subset V_{0}
and
W_{1}\subset W_{0}
of codimension at most
t
such that
u.\beta
is linear on
V_{1}\times W_{1}
and hence on every product
(V_{1}+v)\times(W_{1}+w)
. Let
U_{1}
be the subspace generated by
u
and write
U_{1}^{\perp}
for the subspace
\{v:u.v=0\}
. (Any complementary subspace would do here, but for notational purposes it is more convenient to choose a specific one.) Let
P_{1}
and
Q_{1}
be the projections to
U_{1}
and
U_{1}^{\perp}
arising from the decomposition
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}=U_{1}+U_{1}^{\perp}
. So far we know that
P_{1}\circ\beta
is linear on every product
(V_{1}+v)\times(W_{1}+w)
.

Now let
\beta_{1}
be the restriction of
Q_{1}\circ\beta
to
V_{1}\times W_{1}
. If
\beta_{1}
has rank at least
t
, then we can choose decompositions
X=X_{0}+X_{1}
and
Y=Y_{0}+Y_{1}
with
X_{1}=V_{1}
and
Y_{1}=W_{1}
. To see why this works, pick
(v,w,z)\in V_{1}\times W_{1}\times Z
. Then
B_{v,w,z}
is the set of all
(v+x_{1},w+y_{1})
such that
(x_{1},y_{1})\in X_{1}\times Y_{1}
and
\beta(v+x_{1},w+y_{1})=z
. But

\beta(v+x_{1},w+y_{1})=\beta_{1}(x_{1},y_{1})+(P_{1}\circ\beta)(x_{1},y_{1})+\beta(x_{1},w)+\beta(v,y_{1})+\beta(v,w).

The restriction of
P_{1}\circ\beta
to
X_{1}\times Y_{1}
is linear, as are the last three terms, so the rank of the restriction of
\beta
to
(X_{1}+v)\times(Y_{1}+u)
is equal to the rank of
\beta_{1}
, which is at least
t
. Therefore, for each
z
the set
B_{v,w,z}
is of rank at least
t
, by definition.

Suppose that at the
r
th stage of this process we have found subspaces
V_{r}\subset X
and
W_{r}\subset Y
of codimension at most
tr
and a subspace
U_{r}\subset\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
of dimension
r
such that, writing
P_{r}
and
Q_{r}
for the projections to
U_{r}
and
U_{r}^{\perp}
, we have that
P_{r}\circ\beta
is linear on every product
(V_{r}+v)\times(W_{r}+w)
. If the restriction of
\beta_{r}=Q_{r}\circ\beta
to
V_{r}\times W_{r}
has rank at least
t
, then we are done, by the argument just given when
r=1
. Otherwise, there exist
u\in U_{r}^{\perp}
and subspaces
V_{r+1}\subset V_{r}
and
W_{r+1}\subset W_{r}
of codimension at most
t
such that the restriction of
u.\beta_{r}
to
V_{r+1}\times W_{r+1}
has rank at most
t
. Since
P_{r}\circ\beta
is also linear when restricted to
V_{r+1}\times W_{r+1}
, it follows that
u.\beta
is linear when restricted to
V_{r+1}\times W_{r+1}
. Therefore, if we set
U_{r+1}=U_{r}+\langle u\rangle
and define
P_{r+1}
and
Q_{r+1}
to be the projections to
U_{r+1}
and
U_{r+1}^{\perp}
, we have obtained subspaces
V_{r+1}\subset X
and
W_{r+1}\subset Y
of codimension at most
t(r+1)
and a subspace
U_{r+1}\subset\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
of dimension
r+1
such that
P_{r+1}\circ\beta
is linear on every product
(V_{r+1}+v)\times(W_{r+1}+w)
.

This proves the inductive step. The induction stops either with some
\beta_{r}
such that
Q_{r}\circ\beta_{r}
has rank at least
t
when restricted to
V_{r}\times W_{r}
or with
r=k
. But
Q_{k}
will be the zero map, so by our convention if
r=k
then
Q_{r}\circ\beta_{r}
has infinite rank.
∎

5.2.
Some of the main consequences of high rank

As we have already mentioned, there are several useful statements concerning linear maps that have analogues for bilinear maps that are not true in general but are true when the bilinear maps have high rank. For example, if
\alpha:\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
is a surjective linear map, then the level sets
\{x:\alpha x=z\}
all have density
p^{-k}
, whereas if
\beta:\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}\times\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
is a surjective bilinear map, the level sets
\{(x,y):\beta(x,y)=z\}
may have quite different sizes. An instance of this is the map

(x,y)\mapsto(x_{1}y_{1},\dots,x_{k}y_{k}).

This takes the value
(0,0,\dots,0)
with probability
((2p-1)/p^{2})^{k}
, so the density of the corresponding level set is significantly greater than the average of
p^{-k}
.

The next two lemmas encapsulate what we can get from a high-rank assumption. (The second can be deduced from the first, but with a slightly worse bound.)

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
X,Y
and
Z
be finite-dimensional vector spaces over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, with
\dim Z=k
, let
\beta:X\times Y\to Z
be a bi-affine map of rank
t
, and let
U\subset X
and
V\subset Y
be subspaces of codimensions
u
and
v
. Then if
x
is chosen uniformly at random from
U
, the probability that the restriction of the linear map
\beta_{x\bullet}:y\mapsto\beta(x,y)
to
W
is a surjection is at least
1-p^{u+v+k-t}
.

Proof.

Let
\beta
be given by the formula

\beta(x,y)=(T_{1}x.y+a_{1}.y+x.b_{1}+\lambda_{1},\dots,T_{k}x.y+a_{k}.y+x.b_{k}+\lambda_{k}),

where
T_{1},\dots,T_{k}
are linear maps from
X
to
Y
,
a_{1},\dots,a_{k}
are elements of
Y
,
b_{1},\dots,b_{k}
are elements of
X
, and
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{k}
are elements of
\mathbb{F}_{p}
. The rank assumption is that every non-trivial linear combination of the
T_{i}
has rank at least
t
.

Let
V^{\perp}\subset Y
be the annihilator of
V
(with respect to the dot product), and let
q:Y\to Y/V^{\perp}
be the quotient map. Then the restriction of
\beta_{x\bullet}
is a surjection if and only if the vectors
q(T_{i}x+a_{i})
are linearly independent in
Y/V^{\perp}
.

Let
\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{k}
be scalars, not all zero. Then by hypothesis the map
\sum_{i}\mu_{i}T_{i}
has rank at least
t
, which implies that
q\circ(\sum_{i}\mu_{i}T_{i})
has rank at least
t-v
. Therefore, the probability that
q(\sum_{i}T_{i}x)=-q(\sum_{i}\mu_{i}a_{i})
is at most
p^{v-t}
. Since there are fewer than
p^{k}
choices for the
\mu_{i}
, it follows that the probability that the vectors
q(T_{i}x+a_{i})
are linearly independent is at most
p^{v+k-t}
. Finally, that was the probability for a random
x\in X
. If instead we choose
x
randomly from
U
, this probability increases to at most
p^{u+v+k-t}
, since
U
has density
p^{-u}
in
X
.
∎

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
X
,
Y
and
Z
be finite-dimensional vector spaces over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
and let
\dim Z=k
. Let
(z_{1},\dots,z_{r})
be a sequence of elements of
Z
, and for
1\leq i\leq r
let
\beta_{i}:X\times Y\to Z
be a bi-affine map of rank at least
t
. Then if
x_{1},\dots,x_{r}
are chosen uniformly at random from
X
, the probability that the equations
\beta_{i}(x_{i},y)=z_{i}
have exactly
p^{-rk}|Y|
solutions in
y
is at least
1-p^{rk-t}
.

Proof.

For each
x_{1},\dots,x_{r}
, consider the linear map
\alpha:Y\to Z^{r}
(which depends on
x_{1},\dots,x_{r}
but we suppress this in the notation) given by the formula

\alpha:y\mapsto(\beta_{1}(x_{1},y),\dots,\beta_{r}(x_{r},y)).

For each
i
, let
\beta_{i}
be given by the formula

\beta_{i}(x,y)=(T_{i1}x.y+a_{i1}.y+x.b_{i1}+\lambda_{i1},\dots\dots,T_{ik}x.y+a_{ik}.y+x.b_{ik}+\lambda_{ik}).

The statement that the equations
\alpha(y)=(z_{1},\dots,z_{r})
each have
p^{-rk}|Y|
solutions is equivalent to the statement that
\alpha
has full rank, which in turn is equivalent to the statement that the vectors
T_{ij}x_{i}+a_{ij}
are linearly independent.

It remains to bound the probability that there is a linear dependence between these vectors when
x_{1},\dots,x_{r}
are chosen uniformly at random. We do this by looking at each possible non-zero linear combination, bounding the probability that it is zero, and then using a union bound.

Let
\mu_{ij}\in\mathbb{F}_{p}
for each
1\leq i\leq r
and
1\leq j\leq k
, and suppose that
i^{\prime},j^{\prime}
are such that
\mu_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\neq 0
. Then the rank assumption for
\beta_{i^{\prime}}
implies that the linear map
\sum_{j}\mu_{i^{\prime}j}T_{i^{\prime}j}
has rank at least
t
. It follows that for any fixed choice of
x_{1},\dots,x_{i^{\prime}-1},x_{i^{\prime}+1},\dots,x_{r}
, the probability that
\sum_{ij}\mu_{ij}(T_{i}x_{j}+a_{ij})=0
is at most
p^{-t}
, since there is only one possible value for
(\sum_{j}\mu_{i^{\prime}j}T_{i^{\prime}j})x_{i^{\prime}}
that will cause the equation to be satisfied.

Since there are fewer than
p^{rk}
choices for the
\mu_{ij}
, we obtain the result stated.
∎

We note here a very important consequence of this lemma, which is that a high-rank bilinear Bohr set is quasirandom when thought of as the adjacency matrix of a bipartite graph. To see this, fix a biaffine map
\beta
of dimension
k
, let
B_{z}=\{(x,y):\beta(x,y)=z\}
, and take
r=1
and
r=2
in the lemma above. From the
r=1
case we find that for almost all
x
the set
B_{x\bullet}=\{y:(x,y)\in B\}=\{y:\beta(x,y)=z\}
has density
p^{-k}
and from the
r=2
case we find that for almost all
x_{1},x_{2}
the intersection
B_{x_{1}\bullet}\cap B_{x_{2}\bullet}
has density
p^{-2k}
. This establishes one of the many equivalent conditions for quasirandomness. We shall use this fact later.

We remark here that it is also possible to prove quasirandomness using the analytic definition of rank. This is important too, as it enables one to generalize the above lemma to multilinear maps.

5.3.
A generalized inner product and its basic properties

In this section we examine a generalized inner product that arises naturally in the context of mixed convolutions and 4-arrangements. Using repeated applications of Cauchy-Schwarz, just as one does with the
U^{k}
norms and box norms, we prove a generalized Cauchy-Schwarz inequality for this inner product, which allows us to define a “respecting 4-arrangements norm”, though for our purposes in this paper we shall just need the generalized Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.

Let
G
be a finite Abelian group, let
\mathcal{A}
be its group algebra, and let
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{8}
be functions from
G^{2}
to
\mathcal{A}
. Then we make the definition

[\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\phi_{3},\phi_{4},\phi_{5},\phi_{6},\phi_{7},\phi_{8}]=\langle\mathop{\talloblong\!}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\phi_{3},\phi_{4}),\mathop{\talloblong\!}(\phi_{5},\phi_{6},\phi_{7},\phi_{8})\rangle.

We have in fact already met the quantity on the right-hand side: it appeared in the discussion leading up to Lemma
4.18
.

We also write
[\phi]
as shorthand for
[\phi,\phi,\phi,\phi,\phi,\phi,\phi,\phi]^{1/8}
. Interpreted appropriately,
[\phi]
is a measure of the extent to which
\phi(P)
depends only on the width and height of
P
when
P
is a random vertical parallelogram, and
[\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{8}]
measures the extent to which the product of the
\phi_{i}
over the vertices of a random 4-arrangement is typically close to
\delta_{0}
. (The words “interpreted appropriately” are alluding here to the fact that the distributions over the parallelograms and 4-arrangements must depend appropriately on
\phi
.)

We now state and prove the generalized Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, which is similar to several other such inequalities that have appeared in the literature.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
G
be a finite Abelian group with group algebra
\mathcal{A}
and let
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{8}
be functions from
G^{3}
to
\mathcal{A}
. Then

[\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\phi_{3},\phi_{4},\phi_{5},\phi_{6},\phi_{7},\phi_{8}]\leq[\phi_{1}][\phi_{2}][\phi_{3}][\phi_{4}][\phi_{5}][\phi_{6}][\phi_{7}][\phi_{8}].

Proof.

By the usual Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the definition of the generalized inner product, we have immediately that

[\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{8}]^{2}\leq[\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{4},\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{4}][\phi_{5},\dots,\phi_{8},\phi_{5},\dots,\phi_{8}].

Secondly, if one expands out the definition of the generalized inner product, one can easily verify that

[\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\phi_{3},\phi_{4},\phi_{5},\phi_{6},\phi_{7},\phi_{8}]=[\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\phi_{5},\phi_{6},\phi_{3},\phi_{4},\phi_{7},\phi_{8}],

so we also have the inequality

[\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{8}]^{2}\leq[\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\phi_{5},\phi_{6},\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\phi_{5},\phi_{6}][\phi_{2},\phi_{3},\phi_{7},\phi_{8},\phi_{2},\phi_{3},\phi_{7},\phi_{8}].

We now prove an inequality that is slightly subtler (but still just an application of Cauchy-Schwarz). Let us, very temporarily, define a different mixed convolution by the formula

\{\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\phi_{3},\phi_{4}\}(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3},u_{4})=\mathbb{E}_{x_{1}-x_{2}=x_{3}-x_{4},y}\phi(x_{1},y+u_{1})\phi(x_{2},y+u_{2})^{*}\phi(x_{3},y+u_{3})^{*}\phi(x_{4},y+u_{4}).

Then

[\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{8}]=\langle\{\phi_{1},\phi_{3},\phi_{5},\phi_{7}\},\{\phi_{2},\phi_{4},\phi_{6},\phi_{8}\}\rangle,

and from this and Cauchy-Schwarz we obtain the inequality

[\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{8}]^{2}\leq[\phi_{1},\phi_{3},\phi_{5},\phi_{7},\phi_{1},\phi_{3},\phi_{5},\phi_{7}][\phi_{2},\phi_{4},\phi_{6},\phi_{8},\phi_{2},\phi_{4},\phi_{6},\phi_{8}].

If we now apply the first inequality, then the second inequality to each of the two resulting terms, and the third inequality to each of the four resulting terms of that, we obtain the result claimed.
∎

As usual, it follows easily from the generalized Cauchy-Schwarz inequality above that
[.]
is a norm. To obtain the triangle inequality, one observes that
[g_{1}+g_{2}]^{8}
is a sum of
2^{8}
terms. Each one can be bounded by the inequality, and using those bounds one obtains the inequality
[g_{1}+g_{2}]^{8}\leq([g_{1}]+[g_{2}])^{8}
. This gives the triangle inequality, and the remaining norm properties are very easy.

5.4.
Restricting to a single high-rank bilinear set

Before continuing with the argument, let us briefly recap. We began with a function
\phi
that respected almost all second-order 4-arrangements on a dense set
A
. We obtained from that a function
\psi
that respects almost all first-order 4-arrangements on the mixed convolution
\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mathbbm{1}_{A}
. We then obtained a low-dimensional bilinear map
\beta
such that
\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mathbbm{1}_{A}
is close in
L_{2}
to
P_{\beta}(\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mathbbm{1}_{A})
, where
P_{\beta}
is the averaging projection to the level sets of
\beta
. We also showed that if the
L_{2}
distance between these two functions is sufficiently small, then
\psi
will respect almost all first-order 4-arrangements on
P_{\beta}(\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mathbbm{1}_{A})
as well.

The main result of Subsection
5.1
allows us to create from
\beta
a bilinear Bohr decomposition into high-rank bilinear Bohr sets
B_{v,w,z}
, each of which is contained in a level set of
\beta
. Thus,
P_{\beta}(\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mathbbm{1}_{A})
is constant on each
B_{v,w,z}
. In this section we shall show that
\psi
is a near bihomomorphism on at least one (in fact, most) of the
B_{v,w,z}
. The high-rank condition will play a critical role in our argument, which we shall highlight when we come to it. (We certainly need it for the next lemma, but the main point, which is the one we shall highlight later, is that we cannot do without the lemma.)

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
X,Y
and
Z
be vector spaces over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
and let
\beta:X\times Y\to Z
be a bi-affine map of dimension
k
. Let
X=X_{0}+X_{1}
and
Y=Y_{0}+Y_{1}
be direct-sum decompositions of
X
and
Y
, with
\dim X_{0}=r
and
\dim Y_{0}=s
. For each
(v,w,z)\in X_{0}\times Y_{0}\times Z
let
B_{v,w,z}
be the set
\{(x,y):x_{0}=v,y_{0}=w,\beta(x,y)=z\}
and let
b_{v,w,z}
be the characteristic function of
B_{v,w,z}
. Suppose that the bilinear Bohr decomposition into the sets
B_{v,w,z}
has rank at least
t
.

Let
(u_{1},v_{1},z_{1}),\dots,(u_{8},v_{8},z_{8})
be eight triples in
X_{0}\times Y_{0}\times Z
. If the points
(v_{1},w_{1}),\dots,(v_{8},w_{8})
form a 4-arrangement and
z_{1}-z_{2}-z_{3}+z_{4}=z_{5}-z_{6}-z_{7}+z_{8}
, then

\bigl|[b_{v_{1},w_{1},z_{1}},\dots,b_{v_{8},w_{8},z_{8}}]-p^{-3r-5s-7k}\bigr|\leq p^{-3r-5s}(3p^{2k-t}+p^{k-t}).

Otherwise,
[b_{v_{1},w_{1},z_{1}},\dots,b_{v_{8},w_{8},z_{8}}]=0
.

Proof.

The quantity we are trying to estimate is equal to the probability that the eight vertices of a random 4-arrangement lie in the eight sets
B_{v_{i},w_{i},z_{i}}
(in the right order). To understand the statement of the lemma, it is helpful to define
B_{v,w}
to be the union of all
B_{v,w,z}
, which is the product
(X_{1}+v)\times(Y_{1}+w)
. Then
p^{-3r-5s}
is (as we shall check in more detail below) the probability that each point of a random 4-arrangement lies in the correct set
B_{v_{i},w_{i}}
. The high-rank condition tells us that each set
B_{v,w,z}
should have density approximately
p^{-k}
in
B_{v,w}
, and the term
p^{-7k}
is telling us that the probabilities of landing in the given sets
B_{v_{i},w_{i},z_{i}}
, conditional on having landed in the sets
B_{v_{i},w_{i}}
, are approximately independent, except that once seven of the points are in the right sets, the eighth one is in the right set automatically. Finally, the form of the bound on the right-hand side is not important: what matters is that it can be made significantly smaller than
p^{-3r-5s-7k}
by taking a sufficiently large
t
(which depends on
k
only).

With these remarks out of the way, we can start the proof in earnest. If either of the two conditions on the eight triples
(u_{i},v_{i},w_{i})
fails to hold, then the sets
B_{v_{i},w_{i},z_{i}}
do not support any 4-arrangements. That is because the projection of a 4-arrangement in
X\times Y
to
X_{0}\times Y_{0}
must also be a 4-arrangement, and because
\beta
respects all 4-arrangements.

Now let us consider a random 4-arrangement

(x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{1},y_{1}-h),(x_{2},y_{2}),(x_{2},y_{2}-h),(x_{3},y_{3}),(x_{3},y_{3}-h),(x_{4},y_{4}),(x_{4},y_{4}-h),

which is obtained by choosing a random quadruple
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
with
x_{1}-x_{2}=x_{3}-x_{4}
, a random
h
, and random
y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},y_{4}
. If we exclude
x_{4}
, then the remaining variables are independent, which makes it easy to use Lemma
5.4
to estimate probabilities.

To avoid confusion with indices, write
P
and
Q
for the projections from
X
to
X_{0}
and from
Y
to
Y_{0}
, respectively. Let us begin by estimating the probability that
(x_{1},y_{1})\in B_{v_{1},w_{1},z_{1}}
and
(x_{1},y_{1}-h)\in B_{v_{2},w_{2},z_{2}}
. A necessary condition for this event is that
Qh=w_{1}-w_{2}
, which happens with probability
p^{-s}
. If we condition on this event, then the probability that
(x_{1},y_{1})\in B_{v_{1},w_{1}}
and
(x_{2},y_{1}-h)\in B_{v_{2},w_{2}}
is equal to the probability that
Px_{1}=v_{1}
and
Qy_{1}=w_{1}
, since this automatically implies that
Px_{1}=v_{2}
(because the
(u_{i},v_{i})
form a 4-arrangement). This probability is
p^{-r-s}
. We now claim that if we condition further on the event that
(x_{1},y_{1})\in B_{v_{1},w_{1}}
and
(x_{1},y_{1}-h)\in B_{v_{2},w_{2}}
, then Lemma
5.4
implies that the probability that
\beta(x_{1},y_{1})=z_{1}
and
\beta(x_{1},y_{1}-h)=z_{2}
differs from
p^{-2k}
by at most
p^{2k-t}
.

To justify this last claim, note first that

\beta(x_{1},y_{1})=\beta(v_{1}+(1-P)x_{1},w_{1}+(1-Q)y_{1}),

which has a bi-affine dependence on
(1-P)x_{1}
and
(1-Q)y_{1}
, and

\beta(x_{1},y_{1}-h)=\beta(v_{2}+(1-P)x_{1},w_{2}+(1-Q)(y_{1}-h)),

which has a bi-affine dependence on
(1-P)x_{1}
and
(1-Q)(y_{1}-h)
. Moreover, the two bi-affine maps in question, since they are restrictions of
\beta
to two products of translates of
X_{1}
and
Y_{1}
, are equivalent, and have rank at least
t
by assumption, and the points
(1-Q)y_{1}
and
(1-Q)(y_{1}-h)
are (with the conditionings we have made) independent uniformly distributed random elements of
Y_{1}
. Therefore, by Lemma
5.4
(with the roles of the
x
and
y
variables switched), with probability at least
1-p^{2k-t}
,
y_{1}
and
h
are such that with probability exactly
p^{-2k}
,
\beta(x_{1},y_{1})=z_{1}
and
\beta(x_{1},y_{1}-h)=z_{2}
. For the other
y_{1}
and
h
, this probability cannot differ from
p^{-2k}
by more than 1, and the claim follows. For later reference, let us call the exceptional pairs
(y_{1},h)

bad
pairs.

To summarize what we have shown so far, the probability that
Ph=w_{1}-w_{2}
is
p^{-s}
, and the conditional probability that
(x_{1},y_{1})\in B_{v_{1},w_{1},z_{1}}
and
(x_{1},y_{1}-h)\in B_{v_{2},w_{2},z_{2}}
differs from
p^{-r-s-2k}
by at most
p^{-r-s+2k-t}
.

Since
w_{1}-w_{2}=w_{3}-w_{4}=w_{5}-w_{6}=w_{7}-w_{8}
, the condition on
Ph
gives us the corresponding conditions for each
i=1,2,3,4
. Furthermore, the corresponding conditional probabilities of the pairs of points lying in the sets we want also differ from
p^{-r-s-2k}
by at most
p^{-r-s+2k-t}
.

However, these four conditional probabilities are not independent, for a small reason and a big reason.

The big reason is that once we know that seven of the points belong to their respective sets
B_{v_{i},w_{i},z_{i}}
, we have automatically that the eighth does as well. We shall take account of that in due course. The small reason is that the events that
(y_{i},h)
is a bad pair do not have any reason to be independent. However, this is not much of a problem since only a very small proportion of pairs are bad.

We are ready to complete the proof. We first condition on the event that
Qh=w_{1}-w_{2}
, which, as we have noted, has probability
p^{-s}
. Next, we condition further on the event that
(x_{i},y_{i})\in B_{v_{2i-1},w_{2i-1}}
and
(x_{i},y_{i}-h)\in B_{v_{2i},w_{2i}}
for each
i
. This has probability
p^{-3r-4s}
, since the events that
Px_{i}=v_{2i-1}
occur with probability
p^{-r}
and are independent except that the fourth one follows from the first three, while the events that
Qy_{i}=w_{2i-1}
occur with probability
p^{-s}
and are fully independent.

Having conditioned on this event, we observe that the probability that any of
(y_{1},h)
,
(y_{2},h)
or
(y_{3},h)
is a bad pair is at most
3p^{2k-t}
. Let us also say that
y_{4}
is bad if the probability that
\beta(x_{4},y_{4})=z_{7}
is not
p^{-k}
. Lemma
5.4
tells us that the probability that
y_{4}
is bad is at most
p^{k-t}
. If there is no badness, then for each
i
the probability that
\beta(x_{i},y_{i})=z_{2i-1}
and
\beta(x_{i},y_{i}-h)=z_{2i}
is
p^{-2k}
. For fixed
y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},y_{4},h
the first three of these events are independent. Given that they hold, the probability that
\beta(x_{4},y_{4})=z_{7}
is
p^{-k}
, and given that, it is automatic that
\beta(x_{4},y_{4}-h)=z_{8}
. So the probability that everything is in the right set differs from
p^{-7k}
by at most
3p^{2k-t}+p^{k-t}
. Putting all this together gives the result.
∎

To prepare for the next result, we prove a simple inequality concerning real numbers.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
and
b_{1},\dots,b_{n}
be real numbers with
0\leq a_{i}\leq b_{i}
for every
i
, and suppose that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\geq(1-\eta)\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}
. Then there exists
j
such that
a_{j}\geq(1-2\eta)b_{j}
and
b_{j}\geq\eta n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}
.

Proof.

Let
E=\{j:b_{j}<\eta n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}\}
. If the conclusion of the lemma is false, then for every
j\notin E
we have that
a_{j}<(1-2\eta)b_{j}
. It follows that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}=\sum_{i\in E}a_{i}+\sum_{i\notin E}a_{i}\leq\sum_{i\in E}b_{i}+\sum_{i\notin E}a_{i}<\eta\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}+(1-2\eta)\sum_{i}b_{i}=(1-\eta)\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i},

which contradicts our main assumption.
∎

Corollary 5.8
.

Let
G=\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}
, let
\mathcal{A}
be the group algebra of
G
, and let the sets
B_{v,w,z}
be as in Lemma
5.6
with
X=Y=G
, let
\mu
be a function taking values in
[0,1]
that is constant on each
B_{v,w,z}
, and let
\phi:G^{2}\to\Sigma(\mathcal{A})
be a
(1-\eta)
-bihomomorphism with respect to
\mu
. Let
\xi
be another function taking values in
[0,1]
that is constant on each
B_{v,w,z}
and suppose that
\mathbb{E}\xi=\zeta
. Let
0<\gamma\leq\eta[\mu]^{8}/8
. Suppose that the rank
t
of the bilinear Bohr decomposition satisfies the inequality
p^{-t}\leq\eta p^{-9k}/8
. Then there exists
(v,w,z)
such that
\phi
is a
(1-4\eta)
-bihomomorphism with respect to (the characteristic function of)
B_{v,w,z}
, the value of
\mu
on
B_{v,w,z}
is at least
[\mu]^{8}/2
, and the value of
\xi
on
B_{v,w,z}
is at most
\gamma^{-1}\zeta
.

Proof.

Write
\mu_{v,w,z}
for the value taken by
\mu
on the set
B_{v,w,z}
. Then
\mu=\sum_{v,w,z}\mu_{v,w,z}b_{v,w,z}
, where, once again,
b_{v,w,z}
is the characteristic function of
B_{v,w,z}
.

By Markov’s inequality, for any
\gamma
, if we choose
(x,y)
at random, then the probability that
\xi(x,y)\geq\gamma^{-1}\zeta
is at most
\gamma
. Therefore, if we set
E
to be the set where
\xi(x,y)\geq\gamma^{-1}\zeta
we have that the
L_{1}
norm of the restriction
\nu
of
\mu
to
E
is at most
\gamma
.

Then our hypothesis is that

\|\mathop{\talloblong\!}(\mu\phi)\|_{2}^{2}\geq(1-\eta)\|\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mu\|_{2}^{2},

which is equivalent to the statement

[\mu\phi]^{8}\geq(1-\eta)[\mu]^{8},

where
[\mu]
has the obvious interpretation. (Strictly speaking, the norm
[.]
is defined only for functions from
G^{2}
to
\mathcal{A}
, but we can convert
\mu
into such a function by multiplying it by
\delta_{0}
, for example.) Since
\|\nu\|_{1}\leq\gamma
, it follows that
[\nu\phi]^{8}\geq(1-\eta)[\mu]^{8}-8\gamma\geq(1-2\eta)[\mu]^{8}
.

Writing
\phi_{v,w,z}
for the restriction of
\phi
to
B_{x,y,z}
(or more accurately for the function
b_{v,w,z}\phi
) and expanding both sides, this gives us the statement

\displaystyle\sum_{(v_{1},w_{1},z_{1}),\dots,(v_{8},w_{8},z_{8})\ \hbox{good}}

\displaystyle\mu_{v_{1},w_{1},z_{1}}\dots\mu_{v_{8},w_{8},z_{8}}[\phi_{v_{1},w_{1},z_{1}},\dots,\phi_{v_{8},w_{8},z_{8}}]

\displaystyle\geq(1-2\eta)\sum_{(v_{1},w_{1},z_{1}),\dots,(v_{8},w_{8},z_{8})}\mu_{v_{1},w_{1},z_{1}}\dots\mu_{v_{8},w_{8},z_{8}}[b_{v_{1},w_{1},z_{1}},\dots,b_{v_{8},w_{8},z_{8}}],

where we call
(v,w,z)
good if
B_{v,w,z}
is not a subset of
E
.

By Lemma
5.7
, we can find triples
(v_{i},w_{i},z_{i})_{i=1}^{8}
such that

(1)

[\phi_{v_{1},w_{1},z_{1}},\dots,\phi_{v_{8},w_{8},z_{8}}]\geq(1-4\eta)[b_{v_{1},w_{1},z_{1}},\dots,b_{v_{8},w_{8},z_{8}}]
,

(2)

both sides of the above inequality are non-zero,

(3)

(v_{i},w_{i},z_{i})
is good for
i=1,\dots,8
,

(4)

\mu_{v_{1},w_{1},z_{1}}\dots\mu_{v_{8},w_{8},z_{8}}
is at least
2\eta
times the average over all such products for which the
(v_{i},w_{i})
form a 4-arrangement and
z_{1}-z_{2}-z_{3}+z_{4}=z_{5}-z_{6}-z_{7}+z_{8}
.

Note that because both sides are non-zero, we must have that the pairs
(v_{i},w_{i})
form a 4-arrangement and that
z_{1}-z_{2}-z_{3}+z_{4}=z_{5}-z_{6}-z_{7}+z_{8}
for our chosen triples.

Lemma
5.5
allows us to bound the left-hand side of the inequality in the first condition above by
\prod_{i=1}^{8}[\phi_{v_{i},w_{i},z_{i}}]
, while Lemma
5.6
implies that the right-hand side can be bounded below by
(1-4\eta)p^{-3r-5s}(p^{-7k}-4p^{2k-t})
. It follows that there exists some
i
such that

[\phi_{v_{i},w_{i},z_{i}}]^{8}\geq(1-4\eta)p^{-3r-5s}(p^{-7k}-4p^{2k-t})

and
\mu_{v_{i},w_{i},z_{i}}\geq 2\eta[\mu]^{8}
.

Lemma
5.6
also implies that

[b_{v_{i},w_{i},z_{i}}]^{8}\leq p^{-3r-5s}(p^{-7k}+4p^{2k-t}).

Since
4p^{2k-t}\leq\eta p^{-7k}/2
, it follows that

[\phi_{v_{i},w_{i},z_{i}}]^{8}\geq(1-4\eta)(1-\eta/2)(1+\eta/2)^{-1}[b_{v_{i},w_{i},z_{i}}]^{8}\geq(1-5\eta)[b_{v_{i},w_{i},z_{i}}]^{8},

which gives us that
\phi
is a
(1-5\eta)
homomorphism with respect to
b_{v_{i},w_{i},z_{i}}
.

Finally, since by Lemma
5.6
the numbers
[b_{v_{1},w_{1},z_{1}},\dots,b_{v_{8},w_{8},z_{8}}]
are equal to within a factor of 2, a crude lower bound for the average in (3) above is
[\mu]^{8}/2
. (This is crude because we could improve it by taking account of the fact that many of the
[b_{v_{1},w_{1},z_{1}},\dots,b_{v_{8},w_{8},z_{8}}]
could be zero.)
∎

Now we can see why it was crucial for the sets
B_{v,w,z}
to have high rank (inside their respective sets
B_{v,w}
). In order for the argument to work, it was essential to have a lower bound for
[b_{v_{1},w_{1},z_{1}},\dots,b_{v_{8},w_{8},z_{8}}]
. In general, we can use Lemma
5.5
obtain an upper bound, but there is no matching lower bound. But when the sets
B_{v,w,z}
have high rank, the generalized inner products
[b_{v_{1},w_{1},z_{1}},\dots,b_{v_{8},w_{8},z_{8}}]
that are non-zero are all approximately the same, and this gives us the lower bound we need.

The role of the function
\xi
needs explaining. Recall that the measure that is constant on the sets
B_{v,w,z}
is an
L_{2}
approximation of the mixed convolution of the characteristic function of a set
A^{\prime}\subset G^{2}
. When we come to apply the above lemma, it is important not just that the restriction of
\phi
should be a near bihomomorphism, but also that the mixed convolution
\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mathbbm{1}_{A^{\prime}}
should be approximately constant on the set
B_{v,w,z}
we restrict to. So we shall set
\xi
to be the variance of the restriction. Since the approximation is good on average, it will be good on most sets
B_{v,w,z}
, and we need to make sure that we restrict to one of those sets.

6.
Nearly bilinear functions on high-rank bilinear sets

Let
G
and
H
be finite Abelian groups. Define a map
\phi:G\to H
to be a
(1-\eta)
-homomorphism if the proportion of quadruples
x-y=z-w
for which
\phi(x)-\phi(y)=\phi(z)-\phi(w)
is at least
1-\eta
. It is a well-known and useful fact that if
\phi
is a
(1-\eta)
-homomorphism, then there is a Freiman homomorphism (that is, a 1-homomorphism) that agrees with
\phi
on a subset of
G
of density
1-C\eta
. In the next three sections we shall prove a similar result for
(1-\eta)
-bihomomorphisms defined on bilinear Bohr sets. Whereas the proof of the linear result above is a simple exercise, the proof of the bilinear version is substantially trickier. It also requires the bilinear Bohr set to have high rank, so that it will have quasirandomness properties that we can exploit.

This high-rank assumption is not just convenient, but necessary, as the following simple example shows. (We shall just sketch the proof that the example works.) Let
G=\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}
, let
k
be a positive integer, and let
B\subset G^{2}
be the set
\bigcap_{i,j\leq k}\{(x,y):x_{i}y_{j}=0\}
. Now define
\phi:B\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
by setting
\phi(x,y)=1
if there exists
i\leq k
such that
x_{i}\neq 0
and 0 otherwise.

Note that if
(x,y)\in B
and
x_{i}\neq 0
, then
y_{1}=\dots=y_{k}=0
, so for every
(x,y)\in B
we either have
x_{1}=\dots=x_{k}=0
or
y_{1}=\dots=y_{k}=0
. Let
B_{1}=\{(x,y):x_{1}=\dots=x_{k}=0\}
and
B_{2}=\{(x,y):y_{1}=\dots=y_{k}=0\}
. Then
B=B_{1}\cup B_{2}
. Also, the densities of
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
are
p^{-k}
and the density of
B_{1}\cap B_{2}
is
p^{-2k}
.

There is some choice about how to define a
(1-\eta)
-bihomomorphism in this context, but for any reasonable definition, the function
\phi
we have defined is one for
\eta
roughly comparable to
p^{-k}
. But any 1-bihomomorphism that agrees with
\phi
on a subset of density
1-\eta
will have to agree with
\phi|_{B_{1}}
on most of
B_{1}
and with
\phi|_{B_{2}}
on most of
B_{2}
, which implies easily that it will have to be 0 everywhere on
B_{1}
and 1 everywhere on
B_{2}
, which is not possible.

6.1.
The linear case

Although it is standard, we shall give a proof for the linear case, since the proof will serve as a model for the proof of the bilinear case. Also, our proof is not completely standard, since we phrase it in terms of maps to group algebras.

Let
G
be a group with group algebra
\mathcal{A}
. It will be useful to introduce a notion of distance between elements of
\Sigma(\mathcal{A})
. (Recall that this is the set of non-negative functions in
\mathcal{A}
that sum to 1.) Given
\phi,\psi\in\Sigma(\mathcal{A})
, we define
d(\phi,\psi)
to be
1-\langle\phi,\psi\rangle
.

The distance
d
is not a metric, since
d(\phi,\phi)=1-\|\phi\|_{2}^{2}
does not have to be zero. In this respect, it resembles the Ruzsa distance between two sets. This feature is actually an advantage, as the value of
d(\phi,\phi)
is a useful parameter: if it is small, then it tells us that
\phi
is concentrated at one point, since if
d(\phi,\phi)\leq\epsilon
, then

1-\epsilon\leq\|\phi\|_{2}^{2}\leq\|\phi\|_{1}\|\phi\|_{\infty}=\|\phi\|_{\infty}.

The main reason we call
d
a distance is that it satisfies the triangle inequality.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
\phi,\psi
and
\omega
be elements of
\Sigma(\mathcal{A})
. Then
d(\phi,\omega)\leq d(\phi,\psi)+d(\psi,\omega)
.

Proof.

Note first that if
x,y\in[0,1]
, then
xy\geq x+y-1
(since
(1-x)(1-y)\geq 0
). It follows that

d(\phi,\omega)=1-\langle\phi,\omega\rangle\leq 1-\langle\psi,\phi\omega\rangle\leq 1-\langle\psi,\phi+\omega-1\rangle=2-\langle\psi,\phi+\omega\rangle=d(\phi,\psi)+d(\psi,\omega),

as required.
∎

We also have various obvious symmetry properties, as well as the useful property that
d(fg,h)=d(f,g^{*}h)
. Note also that
d
is bi-affine: that is, if
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}=1
, then
d(\phi,\sum\lambda_{i}\psi_{i})=\sum\lambda_{i}d(\phi,\psi_{i})
. All these properties follow easily from corresponding properties of the inner product.

A further useful lemma is a slight variant of the triangle inequality.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\phi_{3}
and
\phi_{4}
be elements of
\Sigma(\mathcal{A})
. Then

d(\phi_{1}\phi_{2},\phi_{3}\phi_{4})\leq d(\phi_{1},\phi_{3})+d(\phi_{2},\phi_{4}).

Proof.

Observe first that

d(\phi,\psi)=1-\langle\phi,\psi\rangle=1-\langle\phi\psi^{*},\delta_{0}\rangle=d(\phi,\psi,\delta_{0}).

Therefore,

\displaystyle d(\phi_{1}\phi_{2},\phi_{3}\phi_{4})

\displaystyle=d(\phi_{1}\phi_{3}^{*},\phi_{2}^{*}\phi_{4})

\displaystyle\leq d(\phi_{1}\phi_{3}^{*},\delta_{0})+d(\delta_{0},\phi_{2}^{*},\phi_{4})

\displaystyle=d(\phi_{1},\phi_{3})+d(\phi_{2},\phi_{4})

as claimed.
∎

Of course, the above lemma and induction imply that

d(\phi_{1}\dots\phi_{k},\psi_{1}\dots\psi_{k})\leq d(\phi_{1},\psi_{1})+\dots+d(\phi_{k},\psi_{k}).

Another nice fact is the following, which serves as a kind of cancellation law, though we shall not actually need it in this paper.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\phi,\psi
and
\omega
be elements of
\Sigma(\mathcal{A})
with
d(\phi,\psi)\leq 1/2
. Then
d(\phi,\psi)\leq d(\phi\omega,\psi\omega)
.

Proof.

We shall use the fact that
\langle\phi,\psi\rangle=\phi\psi^{*}(0)
for any two functions
\phi,\psi\in\mathcal{A}
, as well as the rather weak inequality
\langle f,g\rangle\leq\|f\|_{1}\|g\|_{1}
. We have that

\displaystyle\langle\phi\omega,\psi\omega\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\phi\psi^{*},\omega\omega^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle=\phi\psi^{*}(0)\omega\omega^{*}(0)+\langle\phi\psi^{*}-\phi\psi^{*}(0)\delta_{0},\omega\omega^{*}-\omega\omega^{*}(0)\delta_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\langle\phi,\psi\rangle\|\omega\|_{2}^{2}+\|\phi\psi^{*}-\langle\phi,\psi\rangle\delta_{0}\|_{1}\|\omega\omega^{*}-\omega\omega^{*}(0)\delta_{0}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\langle\phi,\psi\rangle\|\omega\|_{2}^{2}+(1-\langle\phi,\psi\rangle)(1-\|\omega\|_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle=1-\langle\phi,\psi\rangle+\|\omega\|_{2}^{2}(2\langle\phi,\psi\rangle-1).

Since
d(\phi,\psi)\leq 1/2
,
2\langle\phi,\psi\rangle-1\geq 0
, so this last expression is at most
\langle\phi,\psi\rangle
. This implies the result.
∎

In this language, the definition of a
(1-\eta)
-homomorphism
\phi:G\to\Sigma(\mathcal{A})
becomes that

\mathbb{E}_{x-y=z-w}d(\phi(x)\phi(y)^{*},\phi(z)\phi(w)^{*})\leq\eta.

We can express this in many equivalent ways. For example, it is equivalent to the statement that

d(\mathbb{E}_{x-y=z-w}\phi(x)\phi(y)^{*}\phi(z)^{*}\phi(w),\delta_{0})\leq\eta

and also to the statement that

\mathbb{E}_{u}d(\phi*\phi^{*}(u),\phi*\phi^{*}(u))\leq\eta.

The next lemma tells us that a
(1-\eta)
-homomorphism on a finite Abelian group induces a “difference function” that is an approximate homomorphism in a much stronger sense: it satisfies an approximate additivity law for
all
pairs, and not just most pairs.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
G
be a finite Abelian group with group algebra
\mathcal{A}
and let
\phi:G\to\Sigma(\mathcal{A})
be a
(1-\eta)
-homomorphism. Let
\psi=\phi*\phi^{*}
. Then
d(\psi(u)\psi(v),\psi(u+v))\leq 2\eta
for every
u,v\in G
.

Proof.

This follows from a direct calculation using the basic properties of the distance
d
. Indeed,

\displaystyle d(\psi(u)

\displaystyle\psi(v),\psi(u+v))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x,y,z}d\bigl(\phi(x)\phi(x-u)^{*}\phi(y)\phi(y-v)^{*},\phi(z)\phi(z-u-v)^{*}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x,y,z}d\bigl(\phi(x)\phi(y)\phi(z)^{*},\phi(x-u)\phi(y-v)\phi(z-u-v)^{*}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{x,y,z}d(\phi(x)\phi(y)\phi(z)^{*},\phi(x+y-z))+\mathbb{E}_{x,y,z}d(\phi(x-u)\phi(y-v)\phi(z-u-v)^{*},\phi(x+y-z))

\displaystyle\leq 2\eta,

where the last inequality follows from our starting assumption and the fact that the two terms are both equal to
\mathbb{E}_{x-y=z-w}d(\phi(x)\phi(y)^{*},\phi(z)\phi(w)^{*})
.
∎

Next, let us use a similar argument to prove a more general result (though not a direct generalization).

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
G
be a finite Abelian group with group algebra
\mathcal{A}
and let
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}:G\to\Sigma(\mathcal{A})
be functions such that
\mathbb{E}_{x-y=z-w}d(\phi_{1}(x)\phi_{2}(y)^{*},\phi_{1}(z)\phi_{2}(w)^{*})\leq\eta
. Let
\psi=\phi_{1}\bar{*}\phi_{2}
. Then for every
a,b,c,d
with
a-b=c-d
we have that
d(\psi(a)\psi(b)^{*},\psi(c)\psi(d)^{*})\leq 2\eta
.

Proof.

Note first that our hypothesis can be rewritten

\mathbb{E}_{x-y=z-w}d(\phi_{1}(x)\phi_{1}(y)^{*},\phi_{2}(z)\phi_{2}(w)^{*})\leq\eta,

which says that
\mathbb{E}_{u}d(\phi_{1}*\phi_{1}^{*}(u),\phi_{2}*\phi_{2}^{*}(u))\leq\eta
. This implies that
\mathbb{E}_{u}d(\phi_{i}(u)\phi_{i}(u)^{*},\phi_{i}(u)\phi_{i}(u)^{*})\leq\eta
for
i=1,2
.

Now we proceed directly in a similar way to the previous lemma. Expanding the quantity we wish to bound gives us

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x,y,z,w}d\bigl(\phi_{1}(x)\phi_{2}(x-a)^{*}\phi_{1}(y)^{*}\phi_{2}(y-b),\phi_{1}(z)\phi_{2}(z-c)^{*}\phi_{1}(w)^{*}\phi_{2}(w-d)\bigr).

By Lemma
6.2
(and a slight rearrangement of the second term), this is at most

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x,y,z,w}d\bigl(\phi_{1}(x)\phi_{1}(y)^{*},\phi_{1}(z)\phi_{1}(w)^{*}\bigr)+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x,y,z,w}d\bigl(\phi_{2}(x-a)\phi_{2}(y-b)^{*},\phi_{2}(z-c)\phi_{2}(w-d)^{*}\bigr).

The remarks in the first paragraph give us that these two terms are both at most
\eta
, which proves the lemma.
∎

Using Lemma
6.5
we can quickly obtain a (slight generalization of a) well-known stability result for approximate homomorphisms.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
G
be a finite Abelian group with group algebra
\mathcal{A}
, let
0\leq\eta<1/18
, and let
\phi:G\to\Sigma(\mathcal{A})
be a
(1-\eta)
-homomorphism. Then there exists a 1-homomorphism
\omega:G\to\Sigma(\mathcal{A})
such that
\mathbb{E}_{x}d(\phi(x),\omega(x))\leq 5\eta
.

Proof.

Let
\psi=\phi*\phi^{*}
. Since
\psi=\psi^{*}
, our starting assumption is equivalent to the statement that

\mathbb{E}_{x}d(\phi(x),\phi\bar{*}\psi(x))\leq\eta,

which implies that

\mathbb{E}_{x}d(\phi*\psi^{*}(x),\phi*\psi^{*}(x))\leq\eta.

Setting
\theta=\phi*\psi^{*}
, Lemma
6.5
tells us that
d(\theta(a)\theta(b)^{*},\theta(c)\theta(d)^{*})\leq 2\eta
for every
a,b,c,d
with
a-b=c-d
. This implies that
d(\theta(x),\theta(x))\leq 2\eta
for every
x
, and therefore that
\|\theta(x)\|_{\infty}\geq\|\theta(x)\|_{2}^{2}\geq 1-2\eta
for every
x
. For each
x
, define
\omega(x)
to be
\delta_{u}
, where
u
is the unique element of
G
at which
\theta(x)
takes its maximum value, and observe that
\langle\theta(x),\omega(x)\rangle\geq(1-2\eta)^{2}\geq 1-4\eta
, and therefore that
d(\theta(x),\omega(x))\leq 4\eta
.

By the triangle inequality, it follows that
d(\omega(a)\omega(b)^{*},\omega(c)\omega(d)^{*})\leq 18\eta
for every
a,b,c,d
with
a-b=c-d
. But since each
\omega(x)
is a delta-function and
\eta<1/18
, this implies that
\omega(a)\omega(b)^{*}=\omega(c)\omega(d)^{*}
, so
\omega
is a 1-homomorphism.

Finally, from the inequalities
\mathbb{E}_{x}d(\phi(x),\theta(x))\leq\eta
,
d(\theta(x),\omega(x))\leq 4\eta
, and the triangle inequality, we obtain the bound
\mathbb{E}_{x}d(\phi(x),\omega(x))\leq 5\eta
.
∎

6.2.
Quasirandom sampling

Later we shall need to use a well-known fact about quasirandom bipartite graphs. Since the proof is short, particularly in our context where there are certain regularities that make the argument tidier, we give it in full (slightly disguised, so that bipartite graphs are not explicitly mentioned).

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
\alpha>0
and let
A_{1},\dots,A_{m}
be subsets of a finite set
X
. For each
x\in X
, let
B_{x}=\{i:x\in A_{i}\}
, and suppose that the following two conditions are satisfied.

(1)

All but
\epsilon_{1}|X|
of the sets
B_{x}
have density
\alpha
in
[m]
.

(2)

All but
\epsilon_{2}|X|^{2}
of the intersections
B_{x}\cap B_{y}
have density
\alpha^{2}
in
[m]
.

Let
f:X\to[0,1]
be an arbitrary function and let
\eta>0
. Then for all but at most
(2\alpha\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2})m/\theta^{2}
of the sets
A_{i}
we have the inequality

\bigl|\mathbb{E}_{x}\mathbbm{1}_{A_{i}}(x)f(x)-\alpha\mathbb{E}_{x}f(x)\bigr|\leq\theta.

Proof.

This is an easy second-moment argument. Let us write
\beta_{x}
for the density of
B_{x}
and
\beta_{xy}
for the density of
B_{x}\cap B_{y}
. Then

\mathbb{E}_{i}\mathbb{E}_{x}\mathbbm{1}_{A_{i}}(x)f(x)=\mathbb{E}_{x}f(x)\mathbb{E}_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{A_{i}}(x)=\mathbb{E}_{x}f(x)\beta_{x}=\alpha\mathbb{E}_{x}f(x)\pm\epsilon_{1},

and

\mathbb{E}_{i}\bigl(\mathbb{E}_{x}\mathbbm{1}_{A_{i}}(x)f(x)\bigr)^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{x,y}f(x)f(y)\mathbb{E}_{i}\mathbbm{1}_{A_{i}}(x)\mathbbm{1}_{A_{i}}(y)=\mathbb{E}_{x,y}f(x)f(y)\beta_{xy}=\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}_{x,y}f(x)f(y)+\epsilon_{2}.

It follows that the variance of
\mathbb{E}_{x}\mathbbm{1}_{A_{i}}(x)f(x)
(when
i
is chosen uniformly at random) is at most

\alpha^{2}\mathbb{E}_{x,y}f(x)f(y)+\epsilon_{2}-\bigl(\alpha\mathbb{E}_{x}f(x)\pm\epsilon_{1}\bigr)^{2}\leq\epsilon_{2}+2\alpha\epsilon_{1}.

The result now follows from Chebyshev’s inequality.
∎

We can think of the quantity
\mathbb{E}_{x}\mathbbm{1}_{A_{i}}(x)f(x)
as an attempt to estimate
\mathbb{E}_{x}f(x)
by sampling some of its values. (Of course, one would have to divide by
\alpha
to get the actual estimate.) The lemma is saying that if
A_{1},\dots,A_{m}
satisfy a certain quasirandomness condition, then no matter what the function
f
is, almost all samples will give a good estimate. This basic principle will be essential to our later argument.

6.3.
The bilinear case: obtaining linearity almost everywhere in the first variable

Our aim in this subsection will be to prove that if
\phi
is a
(1-\eta)
-bihomomorphism on a high-rank bilinear Bohr set
B
of codimension
k
, then the mixed convolution
\psi=\mathop{\talloblong\!}\phi
has the property that
d\bigl(\psi(w_{1},h)\psi(w_{2},h),\psi(w_{1}+w_{2},h)\bigr)
is almost everywhere bounded above by a multiple of
\eta
. It is very important that the exceptional set of bad triples
(w_{1},w_{2},h)
is not just small in the way that
\eta
is small, but is significantly smaller than
p^{-k}
, assuming that the rank is large enough. So we cannot prove the result by a simple averaging argument – a genuine use has to be made of the high-rank assumption on
B
.

We first prove two lemmas that are needed in order to establish what the trivial upper bounds are for certain quantities and thereby understand what it is for various expressions to count as small.

To avoid unduly repetitive statements of lemmas, let us decide for the rest of this section that
G=\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}
, that
\mathcal{A}
is a group algebra, that
B\subset G^{2}
is a bilinear Bohr set of codimension
k
and rank
t
, and that
\eta
is a positive constant. Also,
\beta:G^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
will be the bi-affine map used to define
B
. That is,
B=\{(x,y):\beta(x,y)=0\}
.

The following definitions will help to make our arguments more concise.

Definition
.

We shall say that a sequence of points
x_{1},\dots,x_{r}
is
x-normal
for a bi-affine map
\beta
if the proportion of
y
such that
\beta(x_{1},y)=\dots=\beta(x_{r},y)=0
is exactly
p^{-rk}
. Similarly, a sequence
y_{1},\dots,y_{r}
is
y-normal
for
\beta
if the proportion of
x
such that
\beta(x,y_{1})=\dots=\beta(x,y_{k})=0
is exactly
p^{-rk}
.

Lemma
5.4
tells us that a random sequence
(x_{1},\dots,x_{r})
is x-normal for
\beta
with probability at least
1-p^{rk-t}
, and similarly for y-normality. We shall use this many times.

Definition
.

Let the
vertical difference set

B^{\prime}
be the set
\{(x,h):\exists y\ (x,y),(x,y+h)\in B\}
and let the
vertical derivative

\beta^{\prime}
of
\beta
be the function
\beta^{\prime}(x,h)=\beta(x,y+h)-\beta(x,y)
. Define the
mixed difference set

B^{\prime\prime}
to be
\{(w,h):\exists x\ (x,h),(x+w,h)\in B^{\prime}\}
and the
mixed derivative

\beta^{\prime\prime}
of
\beta
by
\beta^{\prime\prime}(w,h)=\beta^{\prime}(x+w,h)-\beta^{\prime}(x,h)
.

Note that
\beta^{\prime}
is well-defined since
\beta
is affine in each variable separately, and therefore
B^{\prime}=\{(x,h):\beta^{\prime}(x,h)=0\}
. Note also that
B^{\prime}_{x\bullet}=B_{x\bullet}-B_{x\bullet}
for each
x\in G
. Since
\beta^{\prime}
is also bi-affine, it follows that
B^{\prime\prime}=\{(w,h):\beta^{\prime\prime}(w,h)=0\}
as well. Expanding the definition of
\beta^{\prime\prime}(w,h)
, one sees that it is equal to

\beta(x+w,y+h)-\beta(x+w,y)-\beta(x,y^{\prime}+h)-\beta(x,y^{\prime})

for some
x,y,y^{\prime}
.

If a parallelogram
(x,y),(x,y+h),(x+w,y^{\prime}),(x+w,y^{\prime}+h)
has all its vertices in
B
, then we find that

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime\prime}(w,h)

\displaystyle=\beta^{\prime}(x+w,h)-\beta^{\prime}(x,h)

\displaystyle=\beta(x+w,y^{\prime}+h)-\beta(x+w,y^{\prime})-\beta(x,y+h)+\beta(x,y)

\displaystyle=0.

The converse is true if
h
is y-normal for
\beta^{\prime}
and the rank
t
of
\beta
is greater than
2k
. Indeed, if
\beta^{\prime\prime}(w,h)=0
, we have that
\beta^{\prime}(x+w,h)-\beta^{\prime}(x,h)=0
for every
x
. By the normality of
h
we can find
x
such that
\beta^{\prime}(x,h)=0
, which gives us that both
\beta^{\prime}(x,h)
and
\beta^{\prime}(x+w,h)
are 0. Moreover, the set of
x
with this property has density
p^{-k}
. Since
\beta
has rank
t
, the probability that
x
and
x+w
are both normal for
\beta
is at least
1-p^{k-t}>1-p^{-k}
, so we can find
x
such that
\beta^{\prime}(x,h)=\beta^{\prime}(x+w,h)=0
and
x
and
x+w
are normal for
\beta
. But then we can find
y
and
y^{\prime}
such that
\beta(x,y)=\beta(x+w,y^{\prime})=0
, which implies that
\beta(x,y+h)=\beta(x+w,y^{\prime}+h)=0
, giving us a parallelogram of width
w
and height
h
in
B
.

Thus, for high-rank bilinear Bohr sets
B
, the set of pairs
(w,h)
such that
B
contains a parallelogram of width
w
and height
h
is contained in
B^{\prime\prime}
and contains almost all of
B^{\prime\prime}
.

In the bounds that follow, we shall write
a\pm b
for a number that lives in the interval
[a-b,a+b]
.

Lemma 6.8
.

Suppose that
h
is y-normal for
\beta^{\prime}
and that
\beta^{\prime\prime}(w,h)=0
. Then the probability that a random 4-arrangement of height
h
and width
w
has all its vertices in
B
is
p^{-6k}\pm 4p^{k-t}
.

Proof.

We wish to estimate the probability that the points
(x_{1},y_{1})
,
(x_{1},y_{1}+h)
,
(x_{1}+w,y_{2})
,
(x_{1}+w,y_{2}+h)
,
(x_{2},y_{3})
,
(x_{2},y_{3}+h)
,
(x_{2}+w,y_{4})
and
(x_{2}+w,y_{4}+h)
all belong to
B
when the parameters
x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},y_{4}
are chosen independently at random.

Since
h
is y-normal for
\beta^{\prime}
, the probability that
\beta^{\prime}(x_{1},h)=\beta^{\prime}(x_{2},h)=0
is
p^{-2k}
, and since
\beta^{\prime\prime}(w,h)=0
, this event implies that

\beta^{\prime}(x_{1},h)=\beta^{\prime}(x_{2},h)=\beta^{\prime}(x_{1}+w,h)=\beta^{\prime}(x_{2}+w,h)=0.

[formula]

If
x_{1}
,
x_{1}+w
,
x_{2}
and
x_{2}+w
are all x-normal for
\beta
, which is the case with probability at least
1-4p^{k-t}
, then the probability (given those values of
x_{1},x_{2}
) that

\beta(x_{1},y_{1})=\beta(x_{1}+w,y_{2})=\beta(x_{2},y_{3})=\beta(x_{2}+w,y_{4})=0

[formula]

is
p^{-4k}
, and otherwise it lies in
[0,1]
. But the points we wish to be in
B
are in
B
if and only if (1) and (2) both hold, and our argument shows that the probability that they both hold is
p^{-6k}\pm 4p^{k-t}
.
∎

Corollary 6.9
.

Suppose that
t\geq 7k
. Then the probability that a random 4-arrangement has all its vertices in
B
is
p^{-7k}\pm 6p^{-t}
.

Proof.

Let the points be as in Lemma
6.8
, but this time with
w
and
h
varying rather than fixed. For each
w
, if
w
is x-normal for
\beta^{\prime\prime}
, then the probability that
\beta^{\prime\prime}(w,h)=0
is
p^{-k}
. It follows that the probability over all
(w,h)
that
\beta^{\prime\prime}(w,h)=0
is
p^{-k}\pm p^{k-t}
. Also, the probability that
h
fails to be y-normal for
\beta^{\prime}
is at most
p^{k-t}
.

If
\beta^{\prime\prime}(w,h)\neq 0
then the conditional probability that the 4-arrangement is in
B
is zero. Putting all this together and using Lemma
6.8
, we may deduce that the probability that all the points are in
B
is

(p^{-k}\pm p^{k-t})(p^{-6k}\pm 5p^{k-t})=p^{-7k}\pm 6p^{-t},

as claimed.
∎

We shall also need a variant of the above results that applies to slightly more elaborate structures.

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
h_{0}=0
and let the sequence
(h_{1},\dots,h_{r})
be y-normal for
\beta^{\prime}
and let
w,x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}
and
y_{4}
be chosen independently at random from
G
. Then the probability that all the points
(x_{1},y_{1}+h_{i})
,
(x_{1}+w,y_{2}+h_{i})
,
(x_{2},y_{3}+h_{i})
and
(x_{2}+w,y_{4}+h_{i})
belong to
B
is
p^{-(3r+4)k}\pm 4p^{k-t}
.

Proof.

The sequence
(h_{1},\dots,h_{r})
is also y-normal for
\beta^{\prime\prime}
, so the probability that
\beta^{\prime\prime}(w,h_{i})=0
for every
i
is
p^{-rk}
. This is a necessary condition for all the points to be in
B
. Let us now condition on this event.

The probability that
\beta^{\prime}(x_{1},h_{i})=\beta^{\prime}(x_{2},h_{i})=0
for every
i\geq 1
is
p^{-2rk}
, and if all those events hold, then since
\beta^{\prime\prime}(w,h_{i})=0
we also have that
\beta^{\prime}(x_{1}+w,h_{i})=\beta^{\prime}(x_{2}+w,h_{i})=0
, which is again a necessary condition.

If the above conditions hold, then a necessary and sufficient further condition for all the points to be in
B
is that the points
(x_{1},y_{1})
,
(x_{1}+w,y_{2})
,
(x_{2},y_{3})
and
(x_{2}+w,y_{4})
belong to
B
. The probability that the points
x_{1},x_{1}+w,x_{2}
and
x_{2}+w
are all x-normal for
\beta
is at least
1-p^{4k-t}
, and if they are all x-normal for
\beta
, then the probability that those four points belong to
B
is
p^{-4k}
. The result follows.
∎

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
w_{1},w_{2},h
be such that
\beta^{\prime\prime}(w_{1},h)=\beta^{\prime\prime}(w_{2},h)=0
and
h
is y-normal for
\beta^{\prime}
. Let
(P_{1},P_{2},P_{3})
be a randomly chosen triple of vertical parallelograms of widths
w_{1},w_{2}
and
w_{1}+w_{2}
and heights all equal to
h
. Then the probability that all the points of
P_{1},P_{2}
and
P_{3}
are in
B
is
p^{-9k}\pm 6p^{k-t}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Lemma
6.8
. This time necessary and sufficient conditions for the three parallelograms to lie in
B
are that

\beta^{\prime}(x_{1},h)=\beta^{\prime}(x_{1}+w_{1},h)=\beta^{\prime}(x_{2},h)=\beta^{\prime}(x_{2}+w_{2},h)=\beta^{\prime}(x_{3},h)=\beta^{\prime}(x_{3}+w_{1}+w_{2},h)=0

[formula]

and that

\beta(x_{1},y_{1})=\beta(x_{1}+w_{1},y_{2})=\beta(x_{2},y_{3})=\beta(x_{2}+w_{2},y_{4})=\beta(x_{3},y_{5})=\beta(x_{3}+w_{1}+w_{2},y_{6})=0.

[formula]

By our hypothesis on
w_{1},w_{2}
and
h
, the first string of equalities holds if and only if

\beta^{\prime}(x_{1},h)=\beta^{\prime}(x_{2},h)=\beta^{\prime}(x_{3},h)=0,

which is true with probability
p^{-3k}
, by the y-normality of
h
.

With probability at least
