1-6p^{k-t}
, all of
x_{1},x_{1}+w_{1},x_{2},x_{2}+w_{2},x_{3}
and
x_{3}+w_{3}
are x-normal for
\beta
, and if they are, then the probability that (2) holds is
p^{-6k}
. It follows that the probability that all three parallelograms are in
B
is
p^{-9k}\pm 6p^{k-t}
, as claimed.
∎

Before we move to the main results of this subsection, it will be helpful to generalize the notion of distance. Up to now we have talked just about distances between elements of
\Sigma(\mathcal{A})
– that is, non-negative functions that sum to 1. We shall now extend the definition so that it applies to all real-valued functions, though we shall apply it only for functions that take non-negative values. The obvious way to do this is the unique way that makes the distance bilinear: that is,

d(f,g)=\Bigl(\sum_{x}f(x)\Bigr)\Bigl(\sum_{y}g(y)\Bigr)-\langle f,g\rangle,

where as before we define the inner product using sums. This is indeed the definition we shall adopt, but we caution that the function is even less like a conventional distance than before, since now the triangle inequality ceases to hold. An easy way to see this is to observe that every function has distance zero from the zero function. However, we shall be careful to apply the triangle inequality only to functions that belong to
\Sigma(\mathcal{A})
.

Using this notion of distance, we can reformulate the definition of a
(1-\eta)
-bihomomorphism. Previously, we said that a function
\phi:G^{2}\to\Sigma(\mathcal{A})
was a
(1-\eta)
-bihomomorphism with respect to a function
\mu:G^{2}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
if

\langle\mathop{\talloblong\!}(\mu\phi),\mathop{\talloblong\!}(\mu,\phi)\rangle\geq(1-\eta)\langle\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mu,\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mu\rangle,

where we defined
\langle\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\rangle
to be
\mathbb{E}_{w,h}\langle\psi_{1}(w,h),\psi_{2}(w,h)\rangle
. For a function
f\in\Sigma(\mathcal{A})
, write
\sigma f
for the sum of the values of
f
. It is an easy exercise to check that for each
(w,h)
, we have that

\sigma(\mathop{\talloblong\!}(\mu\phi)(w,h))=\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mu(w,h).

Therefore,
\phi
is a
(1-\eta)
-bihomomorphism with respect to
\mu
if and only if

d(\mathop{\talloblong\!}(\mu\phi),\mathop{\talloblong\!}(\mu\phi))\leq\eta\langle\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mu,\mathop{\talloblong\!}\mu\rangle,

where the expression on the left-hand side is shorthand for
\mathbb{E}_{w,h}d(\mathop{\talloblong\!}(\mu\phi)(w,h),\mathop{\talloblong\!}(\mu\phi)(w,h))
. In the case that interests us most,
\mu
is the characteristic function
b
of the bilinear Bohr set
B
. Then the values of
\phi
outside
B
make no difference, so we are free to replace them by 0. And if we do that, then the inequality becomes

d(\mathop{\talloblong\!}\phi,\mathop{\talloblong\!}\phi)\leq\eta\langle\mathop{\talloblong\!}b,\mathop{\talloblong\!}b\rangle.

The right-hand side is the probability that a random 4-arrangement
(P_{1},P_{2})
lies in
B
, which we have already shown is approximately
p^{-7k}
, while the left-hand side is the expectation of
d(\phi(P_{1}),\phi(P_{2}))
over all 4-arrangements (this distance being zero unless both
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
lie entirely in
B
). So for
\phi
to be a near bihomomorphism, we need the latter expectation to be small compared with
p^{-7k}
.

We are now ready for the first step in the proof of the bilinear stability result. As with the proof of the linear stability result, our basic strategy is to expand and rearrange the expression we wish to bound, making suitable use of the triangle inequality.

We shall use the notation
P(w,h;x,y,y^{\prime})
to stand for the vertical parallelogram

P=\Bigl((x,y),(x,y+h),(x+w,y^{\prime}),(x+w,y^{\prime}+h)\Bigr).

Recall that
\phi(P)
stands for

\phi(x,y)\phi(x,y+h)^{*}\phi(x+w,y^{\prime})^{*}\phi(x+w,y^{\prime}+h).

We shall also define
\phi_{L}(P)
to be
\phi(x,y)\phi(x,y+h)^{*}
and
\phi_{R}(P)
to be
\phi(x+w,y^{\prime})\phi(x+w,y^{\prime}+h)^{*}
, so that
\phi(P)=\phi_{L}(P)\phi_{R}(P)^{*}
. (The letters L and R stand for “left” and “right”.)

Lemma 6.12
.

Let
\phi:G^{2}\to\mathcal{A}
be a function such that
\phi(x,y)\in\Sigma(\mathcal{A})
if
(x,y)\in B
and
\phi(x,y)=0
otherwise. Let
w_{1},w_{2}
and
h
be such that
\beta^{\prime\prime}(w_{1},h)=\beta^{\prime\prime}(w_{2},h)=0
, and suppose that
h
is y-normal for
\beta^{\prime}
. Then

d\bigl(\psi(w_{1},h)\psi(w_{2},h),\psi(w_{1}+w_{2},h)\bigr)\leq 2p^{-2k}\mathbb{E}_{w}d(\psi(w,h),\psi(w,h))\pm 7p^{2k-t}.

Proof.

Let
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},y_{1},\dots,y_{7}
be chosen at random. Let
P_{1}=P(w_{1},h;x_{1},y_{1},y_{2})
,
P_{2}=P(w_{2},h;x_{2},y_{3},y_{4})
and
P_{3}=P(w_{1}+w_{2},h;x_{3},y_{5},y_{6})
. Then the triple
(P_{1},P_{2},P_{3})
is uniformly distributed over all triples of vertical parallelograms of widths
w_{1},w_{2}
and
w_{1}+w_{2}
and heights all equal to
h
. It follows that

d\bigl(\psi(w_{1},h)\psi(w_{2},h),\psi(w_{1}+w_{2},h)\bigr)=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\\
y_{1},\dots,y_{7}\end{subarray}}d\bigl(\phi(P_{1})\phi(P_{2}),\phi(P_{3})\bigr).

So far, the parameter
y_{7}
has played no role, but now we define four further parallelograms, namely
P_{4}=P(w_{2}+x_{2}-x_{1},h;x_{1},y_{1},y_{4})
,
P_{5}=P(w_{2}+x_{2}-x_{1},h;x_{3},y_{5},y_{7})
,
P_{6}=P(x_{2}-x_{1}-w_{1},h;x_{1}+w_{1},y_{2},y_{3})
and
P_{7}=P(x_{2}-x_{1}-w_{1},h;x_{3}+w_{1}+w_{2},y_{6},y_{7})
. Note that the pairs
(P_{4},P_{5})
and
(P_{6},P_{7})
are uniformly distributed amongst all 4-arrangements of height
h
. Note also that the right edges of
P_{5}
and
P_{7}
coincide, since
x_{3}+(w_{2}+x_{2}-x_{1})=x_{3}+w_{1}+w_{2}+(x_{2}-x_{1}-w_{1})
. Let us write
E
for this common edge: that is, for the pair of points
\bigl((x_{2}+x_{3}-x_{1}+w_{2},y_{7}),(x_{2}+x_{3}-x_{1}+w_{2},y_{7}+h)\bigr)
, and
\phi(E)
as shorthand for
\phi(x_{2}+x_{3}-x_{1}+w_{2},y_{7})\phi(x_{2}+x_{3}-x_{1}+w_{2},y_{7}+h)^{*}
.

Now for all choices of the parameters,

d\Bigl(\phi(P_{1})\phi(P_{2}),\phi(P_{3})\Bigr)=d\Bigl(\phi_{L}(P_{1})\phi_{R}(P_{2})^{*}\phi_{L}(P_{3})^{*},\phi_{R}(P_{1})\phi_{L}(P_{2})^{*}\phi_{R}(P_{3})^{*}\Bigr).

If we know that all of
P_{1},P_{2},P_{3}
and
E
belong to
B
, then the above identity and the triangle inequality give us that

\displaystyle d\Bigl(\phi(P_{1})\phi(P_{2}),\phi(P_{3})\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq d\Bigl(\phi_{L}(P_{1})\phi_{R}(P_{2})^{*}\phi_{L}(P_{3})^{*},\phi(E)^{*}\Bigr)+d\Bigl(\phi(E)^{*},\phi_{R}(P_{1})\phi_{L}(P_{2})^{*}\phi_{R}(P_{3})^{*}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=d\Bigl(\phi_{L}(P_{1})\phi_{R}(P_{2})^{*},\phi_{L}(P_{3})\phi(E)^{*}\Bigr)+d\Bigl(\phi_{R}(P_{1})\phi_{L}(P_{2})^{*},\phi_{R}(P_{3})\phi(E)^{*}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=d\Bigl(\phi(P_{4}),\phi(P_{5})\Bigr)+d\Bigl(\phi(P_{6}),\phi(P_{7})\Bigr),

where the last equality follows from the constructions of
P_{4},P_{5},P_{6}
and
P_{7}
. (For example, since
x_{1}+(w_{2}+x_{2}-x_{1})=x_{2}+w_{2}
, we see that
P_{4}
is made out of the left edge of
P_{1}
and the right edge of
P_{2}
.)

Let us write
\mathbf{x}
for
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
and
\mathbf{y}
for
(y_{1},\dots,y_{7})
and
b(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
for the function that is 1 if all of
P_{1},\dots,P_{7}
are in
B
and 0 otherwise. Then the above calculations prove that

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}b(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})d\Bigl(\phi(P_{1})\phi(P_{2}),\phi(P_{3})\Bigr)\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}b(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})d\Bigl(\phi(P_{4}),\phi(P_{5})\Bigr)+\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}b(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})d\Bigl(\phi(P_{6}),\phi(P_{7})\Bigr).

We now look carefully at the two sides of this inequality. We can rewrite the left-hand side as

\mathbb{E}_{(P_{1},P_{2},P_{3})}d\Bigl(\phi(P_{1})\phi(P_{2}),\phi(P_{3})\Bigr)\mathbb{E}\bigl[b(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})|(P_{1},P_{2},P_{3})\bigr].

The first expectation is over all triples with the appropriate weights and heights, and the conditional expectation is the expectation of
b(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
over all choices of
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
that are consistent with the given triple
(P_{1},P_{2},P_{3})
.

It is not hard to work out what the conditional expectation is, since the triple
(P_{1},P_{2},P_{3})
determines and is determined by the parameters
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},y_{1},\dots,y_{6}
. So all that is left to choose is
y_{7}
. If
P_{1}
,
P_{2}
and
P_{3}
do not all live in
B
, then
b(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is guaranteed to be 0, and if they do all live in
B
, then the conditional expectation of
b(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is the probability that the edge
E
lives in
B
. But if
P_{1},P_{2}
and
P_{3}
live in
B
, then
\beta^{\prime}(x_{1},h)=\beta^{\prime}(x_{2}+w_{2},h)=\beta^{\prime}(x_{3},h)=0
, which implies that
\beta^{\prime}(x_{2}+x_{3}-x_{1}+w_{2},h)=0
, so we are looking for the probability that
\beta(x_{2}+x_{3}-x_{1}+w_{2},h)=0
. Since
x_{1},x_{2}
and
x_{3}
are chosen uniformly,
x_{2}+x_{3}-x_{1}+w_{2}
is x-normal for
\beta
with probability at least
1-p^{k-t}
. Therefore, if we choose a random triple
(P_{1},P_{2},P_{3})
the conditional expectation is
p^{-k}
with probability at least
1-p^{k-t}
, and since all the distances are between 0 and 1, this proves that the entire expression is equal to

p^{-k}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}d\bigl(\phi(P_{1})\phi(P_{2}),\phi(P_{3})\bigr)\pm p^{k-t}=p^{-k}d\bigl(\psi(w_{1},h)\psi(w_{2},h),\psi(w_{1}+w_{2},h)\bigr)\pm p^{k-t}.

The two terms on the right-hand side can be dealt with similarly. Since there is a symmetry between them, we shall discuss just the first term. We rewrite it in a similar way, this time as

\mathbb{E}_{(P_{4},P_{5})}d\bigl(\phi(P_{4}),\phi(P_{5})\bigr)\mathbb{E}\bigl[b(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})|(P_{4},P_{5})\bigr].

Let us set
x_{2}^{\prime}
to be
x_{2}+w_{2}
. Then the 4-arrangement
(P_{4},P_{5})
determines and is determined by the variables
x_{1},x_{2}^{\prime},x_{3},y_{1},y_{4}
and
y_{7}
. Since
x_{2}=x_{2}^{\prime}-w_{2}
, that means that the only variables that it remains to choose are
y_{2},y_{3}
and
y_{6}
. If
P_{4}
and
P_{5}
are not both in
B
, then
b(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=0
. Otherwise, we have that
\beta^{\prime}(x_{1},h)=\beta^{\prime}(x_{2}^{\prime},h)=\beta^{\prime}(x_{3},h)=0
, and since
\beta^{\prime\prime}(w_{1},h)=\beta^{\prime\prime}(w_{2},h)=0
, it follows that
\beta^{\prime}(x_{1}+w_{1},h)=\beta^{\prime}(x_{2},h)=\beta^{\prime}(x_{3}+w_{1}+w_{2},h)=0
. So we wish to know the probability, given
P_{4}
and
P_{5}
, that
\beta(x_{1}+w_{1},y_{2})=\beta(x_{2},y_{3})=\beta(x_{3}+w_{1}+w_{2},y_{6})=0
.
If
x_{1}+w_{1},x_{2}^{\prime}-w_{2}
and
x_{3}+w_{1}+w_{2}
are x-normal for
\beta
, which happens with probability at least
1-3p^{k-t}
, then this probability is
p^{-3k}
. Therefore, the term we are estimating is equal to

p^{-3k}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}d\bigl(\phi(P_{4}),\phi(P_{5})\bigr)\pm 3p^{k-t}=p^{-3k}\mathbb{E}_{w}d\bigl(\psi(w,h),\psi(w,h)\bigr)\pm 3p^{k-t}.

The term
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}b(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})d\bigl(\phi(P_{6}),\phi(P_{7})\bigr)
can be estimated in a similar way and gives the same result. Therefore, we obtain the estimate

p^{-k}d\bigl(\psi(w_{1},h)\psi(w_{2},h),\psi(w_{1}+w_{2},h)\bigr)=2p^{-3k}\mathbb{E}_{w}d\bigl(\psi(w,h),\psi(w,h)\bigr)\pm 7p^{k-t},

which gives us the inequality we wanted.
∎

We shall be assuming that
\phi
is a
(1-\eta)
-bihomomorphism, so we need a bound in terms of the average of
d\bigl(\psi(w,h^{\prime}),\psi(w,h^{\prime})\bigr)
over all
w,h^{\prime}
. The lemma above has made some progress towards that goal, by allowing us to average over
w
. But now we must replace the fixed
h
by an average. This we shall do in a similar way, but there will be an interesting complication.

Given a function
\phi:G^{2}\to\mathcal{A}
and a parallelogram
P=P(w,h;x,y,y^{\prime})
, we shall write
\phi_{D}(P)
for
\phi(x,y)\phi(x+w,y^{\prime})^{*}
and
\phi_{U}(P)
for
\phi(x,y+h)\phi(x+w,y^{\prime}+h)^{*}
. (The letters D and U stand for “down” and “up”.) We have
\phi(P)=\phi_{D}(P)\phi_{U}(P)^{*}
for every vertical parallelogram
P
.

Lemma 6.13
.

Let
G=\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}
, let
\theta>0
and let
B\subset G^{2}
be a bilinear Bohr set of codimension
k
and rank
t
. Let
\mathcal{A}
be a group algebra and let
\phi:G^{2}\to\mathcal{A}
be a function such that
\phi(x,y)\in\Sigma(\mathcal{A})
if
(x,y)\in B
and
\phi(x,y)=0
otherwise. Then

\mathbb{E}_{w}d\bigl(\psi(w,h),\psi(w,h)\bigr)\leq 2\mathbb{E}_{w^{\prime},h^{\prime}}d\bigl(\psi(w^{\prime},h^{\prime}),\psi(w^{\prime},h^{\prime})\bigr)+2\theta

for all
h
outside a set of density at most
12p^{26k-t}/\theta^{2}
.

Proof.

When expanded, the left-hand side becomes

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}w,x_{1},x_{2}\\
y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},y_{4}\end{subarray}}d\bigl(\phi(P_{1}),\phi(P_{2})\bigr),

where
P_{1}=P(w,h;x_{1},y_{1},y_{2})
and
P_{2}=P(w,h;x_{2},y_{3},y_{4})
.
As in the proof of Lemma
6.12
we begin by decomposing
d\bigl(\phi(P_{1}),\phi(P_{2})\bigr)
and applying the triangle inequality. To do this, we introduce a new variable
h^{\prime}
(just as we introduced a new variable
y_{7}
before), and this time we let
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
be the “horizontal edges”
\bigl((x_{1},y_{1}+h^{\prime}),(x_{1}+w,y_{2}+h^{\prime})\bigr)
and
\bigl((x_{2},y_{3}+h^{\prime}),(x_{2}+w,y_{4}+h^{\prime})\bigr)
, writing
\phi(E_{1})
and
\phi(E_{2})
for
\phi(x_{1},y_{1}+h^{\prime})\phi(x_{1}+w,y_{2}+h^{\prime})^{*}
and
\phi(x_{2},y_{3}+h^{\prime})\phi(x_{2}+w,y_{4}+h^{\prime})^{*}
. Then as long as
P_{1},P_{2},E_{1}
and
E_{2}
are all inside
B
, we have that

\displaystyle d\bigl(\phi(P_{1}),\phi(P_{2})\bigr)

\displaystyle=d\bigl(\phi_{D}(P_{1})\phi_{U}(P_{1})^{*},\phi_{D}(P_{2})\phi_{U}(P_{2})^{*}\bigr)

\displaystyle=d\bigl(\phi_{D}(P_{1})\phi_{D}(P_{2})^{*},\phi_{U}(P_{1})\phi_{U}(P_{2})^{*}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq d\bigl(\phi_{D}(P_{1})\phi_{D}(P_{2})^{*},\phi(E_{1})\phi(E_{2})^{*}\bigr)+d\bigl(\phi(E_{1})\phi(E_{2})^{*},\phi_{U}(P_{1})\phi_{U}(P_{2})^{*}\bigr)

\displaystyle=d\bigl(\phi_{D}(P_{1})\phi(E_{1})^{*},\phi_{D}(P_{2})\phi(E_{2})^{*}\bigr)+d\bigl(\phi(E_{1})\phi_{U}(P_{1})^{*},\phi(E_{2})^{*}\phi_{U}(P_{2})^{*}\bigr)

\displaystyle=d\bigl(\phi(P_{3}),\phi(P_{4})\bigr)+d\bigl(\phi(P_{5}),\phi(P_{6})\bigr),

where we have set
P_{3}=P(w,h^{\prime};x_{1},y_{1},y_{2})
,
P_{4}=P(w,h^{\prime};x_{2},y_{3},y_{4})
,
P_{5}=P(w,h-h^{\prime};x_{1},y_{1}+h^{\prime},y_{2}+h^{\prime})
and
P_{6}=P(w,h-h^{\prime};x_{2},y_{3}+h^{\prime},y_{4}+h^{\prime})
. (Geometrically, we have sliced each of
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
into parallelograms of heights
h^{\prime}
and
h-h^{\prime}
.)

The pairs
(P_{3},P_{4})
and
(P_{5},P_{6})
are uniformly distributed amongst all 4-arrangements, but this, though extremely important to us, is not quite as useful as it might at first look. To see where the complication arises, let us now try to use the above inequality in the way that we used a similar inequality in the proof of Lemma
6.12
. This time we shall set
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2})
and
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},y_{4})
, and we shall let
b(w,h^{\prime},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
equal 1 if all of
P_{1},P_{2},E_{1}
and
E_{2}
are inside
B
and 0 otherwise. Then the above argument gives us that

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{w,h^{\prime},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}b(w,h^{\prime},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})d\bigl(\phi(P_{1}),\phi(P_{2})\bigr)\leq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{w,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}b(w,h^{\prime},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle d\bigl(\phi(P_{3}),\phi(P_{4})\bigr)

\displaystyle+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{w,h^{\prime},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}b(w,h^{\prime},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})d\bigl(\phi(P_{5}),\phi(P_{6})\bigr).

Again we discuss the terms separately, beginning with the left-hand side. For each
(P_{1},P_{2})
, the weight attached to
d\bigl(\phi(P_{1}),\phi(P_{2})\bigr)
is the expectation of
b(w,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
given
(P_{1},P_{2})
. If
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are not both in
B
, this is zero. Otherwise, it is the probability that
(x_{1},y_{1}+h^{\prime})
,
(x_{1}+w,y_{2}+h^{\prime})
,
(x_{2},y_{3}+h^{\prime})
and
(x_{2}+w_{2},y_{3}+h^{\prime})
all belong to
B
when
h^{\prime}
is chosen uniformly at random. (The pair
(P_{1},P_{2})
determines and is determined by the other parameters
w
,
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
.) We are given that
(x_{1},y_{1}),(x_{1}+w,y_{2}),(x_{2},y_{3})
and
(x_{2}+w,y_{4})
belong to
B
, so a necessary and sufficient condition for this is that

\beta^{\prime}(x_{1},h^{\prime})=\beta^{\prime}(x_{1}+w,h^{\prime})=\beta^{\prime}(x_{2},h^{\prime})=\beta^{\prime}(x_{2}+w,h^{\prime})=0.

The last equality here is redundant, since
x_{2}+w=(x_{1}+w)+x_{2}-x_{1}
.

The triple
(x_{1},x_{1}+w,x_{2})
is uniformly distributed in
G^{3}
. Therefore, it is x-normal for
\beta^{\prime}
with probability at least
1-p^{3k-t}
, and whenever it is x-normal for
\beta^{\prime}
, the probability that the equations hold is
p^{-3k}
. It follows that

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{w,h^{\prime},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}b(w,h^{\prime},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})d\bigl(\phi(P_{1}),\phi(P_{2})\bigr)=p^{-3k}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{w,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}d\bigl(\phi(P_{1}),\phi(P_{2})\bigr)\pm p^{3k-t}.

Now let us turn our attention to the term
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{w,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}b(w,h^{\prime},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})d\bigl(\phi(P_{3}),\phi(P_{4})\bigr)
. It is here that something a little curious happens: the 4-arrangement
(P_{3},P_{4})
determines all the parameters
w,h^{\prime},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
, so the expectation of
b(w,h^{\prime},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
given
(P_{3},P_{4})
is equal to the value of
b
for that choice of parameters. If
P_{3}
and
P_{4}
are not both in
B
, then we get zero, as usual, but if
P_{3}
and
P_{4}

are
both in
B
it does
not
follow that
b(w,h^{\prime},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=1
: this happens only if the points
(x_{1},y_{1}+h),(x_{1}+w,y_{2}+h),(x_{2},y_{3}+h)
and
(x_{2}+w,y_{4}+h)
all lie in
B
.

Thus, the term
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{w,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}b(w,h^{\prime},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})d\bigl(\phi(P_{3}),\phi(P_{4})\bigr)
is proportional to the sum of the distances
d\bigl(\phi(P_{3}),\phi(P_{4})\bigr)
over only a small subset of the 4-arrangements
(P_{3},P_{4})
that live in
B
.

This looks like a serious problem, but what rescues us is that the subset is a quasirandom sample. This will allow us to conclude, using Lemma
6.7
, that for almost every
h
we obtain the inequality we were hoping for.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be the set of all 4-arrangements, let
\mathcal{Q}^{B}
be the set of all 4-arrangements that live in
B
, and for each
h
let
\mathcal{Q}^{B}(h)
be the set of all 4-arrangements
(P_{3},P_{4})
in
\mathcal{Q}^{B}
such that if
P_{3}=P(w,h^{\prime};x_{1},y_{1},y_{2})
and
P_{4}=P(w,h^{\prime};x_{2},y_{3},y_{4})
, then the points
(x_{1},h),(x_{1}+w,h)
and
(x_{2},h)
lie in
B^{\prime}
. Note that this implies that the points
(x_{1},y_{1}+h),(x_{1}+w,y_{2}+h)
and
(x_{2},y_{3}+h)
lie in
B
(for example,
\beta(x_{1}+w,y_{2}+h)=\beta(x_{1}+w)+\beta^{\prime}(x_{1},h)
), which in turn implies that
(x_{2}+w,y_{4}+h)
lies in
B
(since if you have seven points of a 4-arrangement then you must have the eighth as well).

If we choose a random 4-arrangement
(P_{3},P_{4})\in\mathcal{Q}
with the parameters as in the previous paragraph, then the triple
(x_{1},x_{1}+w,x_{2})
is uniformly distributed in
G^{3}
, so the probability that
(x_{1},x_{1}+w,x_{2})
is not x-normal for
\beta^{\prime}
is at most
p^{3k-t}
. Therefore, by Lemma
6.9
, the probability that it is not x-normal given that
(P_{3},P_{4})\in\mathcal{Q}^{B}
is at most
2p^{10k-t}
, as long as
p^{-t}\leq p^{-7k}/12
. (With more care, one can improve on this bound, but that does not gain us anything interesting.)

If the triple
(x_{1},x_{1}+w,x_{2})
is x-normal for
\beta^{\prime}
, then the probability that
(x_{1},h),(x_{1}+w,h)
and
(x_{2},h)
belong to
B^{\prime}
is
p^{-3k}
for each
h
. For such 4-arrangements
(P_{3},P_{4})
it follows that if we choose
h
at random, then the probability that
(P_{3},P_{4})\in\mathcal{Q}^{B}(h)
is
p^{-3k}
.

Similarly, if we choose
(P_{3},P_{4})
and
(P_{3}^{\prime},P_{4}^{\prime})
at random, and write
(x_{1}^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime}),(x_{1}^{\prime}+w^{\prime},y_{2}^{\prime})
and
(x_{2}^{\prime},y_{3}^{\prime})
for “the first three points on the bottom row” of
(P_{3}^{\prime},P_{4}^{\prime})
, then the sextuple
(x_{1},x_{1}+w,x_{2},x_{1}^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime}+w^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime})
is uniformly distributed, so has a probability at most
p^{6k-t}
of failing to be x-normal for
\beta^{\prime}
, which rises to at most
4p^{20k-t}
if we condition on both
(P_{3},P_{4})
and
(P_{3}^{\prime},P_{4}^{\prime})
belonging to
\mathcal{Q}^{B}
. If the sextuple
is
x-normal for
\beta^{\prime}
and we choose a random
h
, the probability that
(P_{3},P_{4})
and
(P_{3}^{\prime},P_{4}^{\prime})
both belong to
\mathcal{Q}^{B}(h)
is
p^{-6k}
.

This gives us the conditions we need in order to apply Lemma
6.7
, with
X=\mathcal{Q}^{B}
, the sets
\mathcal{Q}^{B}(h)
as the
A_{i}
, and
\theta
replacing
\eta
. We can set
\epsilon_{1}=2p^{10k-t}
,
\epsilon_{2}=4p^{20-k}
, and
\alpha=p^{-3k}
. Then for all
h
outside a set of density at most
6p^{26-k}/\theta^{2}
we have that

\bigl|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{w,h^{\prime},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}b(w,h^{\prime},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})d\bigl(\phi(P_{3}),\phi(P_{4})\bigr)-p^{-3k}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{w,h^{\prime},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}d\bigl(\phi(P_{3}),\phi(P_{4})\bigr)\bigr|\leq p^{-3k}\theta.

The same argument works for
(P_{5},P_{6})
and gives the same average, so we have shown that

p^{-3k}\mathbb{E}_{w,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}d\bigl(\phi(P_{1}),\phi(P_{2})\bigr)\pm p^{3k-t}\leq 2p^{-3k}\mathbb{E}_{w,h^{\prime},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}d\bigl(\phi(P_{3}),\phi(P_{4})\bigr)+2p^{-3k}\theta

for all
h
outside a set of density at most
12p^{26k-t}/\theta^{2}
, which implies, for all such
h
, that

\mathbb{E}_{w}d\bigl(\psi(w,h),\psi(w,h)\bigr)\leq 2\mathbb{E}_{w^{\prime},h^{\prime}}d\bigl(\psi(w^{\prime},h^{\prime}),\psi(w^{\prime},h^{\prime})\bigr)+2\theta.

This proves the lemma. ∎

Note that in the above lemma there is nothing to stop
\theta
depending on
k
, and indeed it is essential that it should be able to do so, since we need it to be small compared with
p^{-7k}
.

6.4.
The bilinear case: obtaining linearity almost everywhere in the second variable

We shall now prove a similar result to that of the last section, but this time obtaining linearity in the second variable. Since the proof is also similar, we shall present it more concisely.

Lemma 6.14
.

Let
\phi:G^{2}\to\mathcal{A}
be a function such that
\phi(x,y)\in\Sigma(\mathcal{A})
if
(x,y)\in B
and
\phi(x,y)=0
otherwise. Let
w,h_{1}
and
h_{2}
be such that
\beta^{\prime\prime}(w,h_{1})=\beta^{\prime\prime}(w,h_{2})=0
, and suppose that
w
is x-normal for
\beta^{\prime}
. Then for every
\theta\in(0,1]
we have that

d\bigl(\psi(w,h_{1})\psi(w,h_{2}),\psi(w,h_{1}+h_{2})\bigr)\leq 2p^{-2k}\mathbb{E}_{h}d(\psi(w,h),\psi(w,h))+10p^{13k-t}+6\theta p^{-7k}

provided that
w
lies outside a set of density at most
p^{k-t}
and
(h_{1},h_{2})
lies outside a set of density at most
(25p^{16k-t})/\theta^{2}
.

Proof.

Let
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},y_{1},\dots,y_{6}
be chosen at random. Let
P_{1}=P(w,h_{1};x_{1},y_{1},y_{2})
,
P_{2}=P(w,h_{2};x_{2},y_{3},y_{4})
and
P_{3}=P(w,h_{1}+h_{2};x_{3},y_{5},y_{6})
. Then the triple
(P_{1},P_{2},P_{3})
is uniformly distributed over all triples of vertical parallelograms of widths all equal to
w
and heights
h_{1},h_{2}
and
h_{1}+h_{2}
.

Now let
h
be a new parameter and define the following six further parallelograms. Writing
h_{3}
for
h_{1}+h_{2}
, we let
P_{4}=P(w,h_{3}+h;x_{1},y_{1},y_{2})
,
P_{5}=P(w,h_{2}+h;x_{1},y_{1}+h_{1},y_{2}+h_{1})
,
P_{6}=P(w,h_{2}+h;x_{2},y_{3},y_{4})
,
P_{7}=P(w,h;x_{2},y_{3}+h_{2},y_{4}+h_{2})
,
P_{8}=P(w,h_{3}+h;x_{3},y_{5},y_{6})
, and
P_{9}=P(w,h;x_{3},y_{5}+h_{3},y_{6}+h_{3})
. We shall also define the three “horizontal edges”
F_{1}=\bigl((x_{1},y_{1}+h_{3}+h),(x_{1}+w,y_{2}+h_{3}+h)\bigr)
,
F_{2}=\bigl((x_{2},y_{3}+h_{2}+h),(x_{2}+w,y_{4}+h_{2}+h)\bigr)
, and
F_{3}=\bigl((x_{3},y_{5}+h_{3}+h),(x_{3}+w,y_{6}+h_{3}+h)\bigr)
, each of which belongs to two of the six parallelograms above.

We shall now apply the triangle inequality twice. First, note that

\displaystyle d\bigl(\phi(P_{1})\phi(P_{2}),\phi(P_{3})\bigr)

\displaystyle=d\bigl(\phi_{D}(P_{1})\phi_{D}(P_{3})^{*},\phi(P_{2})^{*}\phi_{U}(P_{1})\phi_{U}(P_{3})^{*}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq d\bigl(\phi_{D}(P_{1})\phi_{D}(P_{3})^{*},\phi(F_{1})\phi(F_{3})^{*}\bigr)+d\bigl(\phi(F_{1})\phi(F_{3})^{*},\phi(P_{2})^{*}\phi_{U}(P_{1})\phi_{U}(P_{3})^{*}\bigr)

The first term in the last line is equal to
d\bigl(\phi_{D}(P_{1})\phi(F_{1})^{*},\phi_{D}(P_{3})\phi(F_{3})^{*}\bigr)
, which is equal to
d\bigl(\phi(P_{4}),\phi(P_{8})\bigr)
. The second term is equal to

\displaystyle d\bigl(\phi_{U}(P_{1})

\displaystyle\phi(F_{1})^{*}\phi_{L}(P_{2})^{*},\phi_{U}(P_{3})\phi(F_{3})^{*}\phi_{U}(P_{2})^{*}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq d\bigl(\phi_{U}(P_{1})\phi(F_{1})^{*}\phi_{L}(P_{2})^{*},\phi(F_{2})^{*}\bigr)+d\bigl(\phi(F_{2})^{*},\phi_{U}(P_{3})\phi(F_{3})^{*}\phi_{U}(P_{2})^{*}\bigr)

\displaystyle=d\bigl(\phi_{U}(P_{1})\phi(F_{1})^{*},\phi_{L}(P_{2})\phi(F_{2})^{*}\bigr)+d\bigl(\phi_{U}(P_{2})\phi(F_{2})^{*},\phi_{U}(P_{3})\phi(F_{3})^{*}\bigr)

\displaystyle=d\bigl(\phi(P_{5}),\phi(P_{6})\bigr)+d\bigl(\phi(P_{7}),\phi(P_{9})\bigr).

Putting all this together, we have proved that for every
h
we have the inequality

d\bigl(\phi(P_{1})\phi(P_{2}),\phi(P_{3})\bigr)\leq d\bigl(\phi(P_{4}),\phi(P_{8})\bigr)+d\bigl(\phi(P_{5}),\phi(P_{6})\bigr)+d\bigl(\phi(P_{7}),\phi(P_{9})\bigr).

Note that each pair
(P_{4},P_{8})
,
(P_{5},P_{6})
and
(P_{7},P_{9})
is a 4-arrangement of width
w
. Moreover, for any fixed choice of
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
, each pair is uniformly distributed over all such 4-arrangements when the remaining parameters
h,x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},y_{1},\dots,y_{6}
are chosen independently and uniformly.

Now let us write
\mathbf{x}
for
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
and
\mathbf{y}
for
(y_{1},\dots,y_{6})
and set
b(h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
to be 1 if
P_{1},P_{2},P_{3},F_{1},F_{2}
and
F_{3}
(and hence
P_{4},\dots,P_{9}
) all live in
B
. Then the above inequality implies that

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}

\displaystyle b(h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})d\bigl(\phi(P_{1})\phi(P_{2}),\phi(P_{3})\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}b(h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})d\bigl(\phi(P_{4}),\phi(P_{8})\bigr)+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}b(h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})d\bigl(\phi(P_{5}),\phi(P_{6})\bigr)+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}b(h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})d\bigl(\phi(P_{7}),\phi(P_{9})\bigr).

As in the previous section, we shall now approximately evaluate the two sides of this inequality in order to obtain the statement in the lemma, beginning with the left-hand side.

If
P_{1},P_{2}
and
P_{3}
all belong to
B
, then the probability that
F_{1}
,
F_{2}
and
F_{3}
also belong to
B
is equal to the probability that
\beta^{\prime}(x_{1},h_{3}+h)=\beta^{\prime}(x_{2},h_{2}+h)=\beta^{\prime}(x_{3},h_{3}+h)=\beta^{\prime\prime}(w,h)=0
, since these equations imply that
\beta^{\prime}(x_{1}+w,h_{3}+h)=\beta^{\prime}(x_{2}+w,h_{2}+h)=\beta^{\prime}(x_{3}+w,h_{3}+h)=0
, and then, given that the lower edges of
P_{1},P_{2}
and
P_{3}
belong to
B
we can deduce that
F_{1},F_{2}
and
F_{3}
do as well.

Recall that
w
is x-normal for
\beta^{\prime\prime}
. With probability at least
1-p^{4k-t}
, the sequence
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
is x-normal for
\beta^{\prime}
when we restrict to the
k
-codimensional subspace consisting of those
h
for which
\beta^{\prime\prime}(w,h)=0
, and if it is, then all values of
(\beta^{\prime}(x_{1},h),\beta^{\prime}(x_{2},h),\beta^{\prime}(x_{3},h))
occur with probability
p^{-3k}
. Therefore, the probability that
\beta^{\prime\prime}(w,h)=0
is
p^{-k}
, and given this event and the normality of
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
in the subspace, the probability that
\beta^{\prime}(x_{1},h)=-\beta^{\prime}(x_{1},h_{3})
,
\beta^{\prime}(x_{2},h)=-\beta^{\prime}(x_{2},h_{2})
and
\beta^{\prime}(x_{3},h)=-\beta^{\prime}(x_{3},h_{3})
is
p^{-3k}
. It follows that

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}b(h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})d\bigl(\phi(P_{1})\phi(P_{2}),\phi(P_{3})\bigr)=p^{-4k}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}d\bigl(\phi(P_{1})\phi(P_{2}),\phi(P_{3})\bigr)\pm p^{4k-t}.

Again as in the previous section, it is enough to look at just one term on the right-hand side. Let
(P_{4},P_{8})
be an arbitrary 4-arrangement of width
w
. Note that if we are given the information that
P_{4}=P(w,h_{3}+h;x_{1},y_{1},y_{2})
and
P_{8}=P(w,h_{3}+h;x_{3},y_{5},y_{6})
, then (since
w
,
h_{1}
and
h_{3}
are fixed) the parameters
x_{1},y_{1},y_{2},x_{3},y_{5},y_{6}
and
h
are determined, leaving just the parameters
x_{2},y_{3}
and
y_{4}
to be chosen.

If
\beta^{\prime}(x_{1},h_{1})\neq 0
, then the point
(x_{1},y_{1}+h_{1})
does not belong to
B
, so
b(h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=0
. Similarly, if
\beta^{\prime}(x_{3},h_{3})\neq 0
, then
b(h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=0
. So
\mathbb{E}[b(h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}|(P_{4},P_{8})]=0
unless
\beta^{\prime}(x_{1},h_{1})=\beta^{\prime}(x_{3},h_{3})=0
, so once again the best we can hope for is a quasirandom sample, this time of the 4-arrangements of width
w
that live in
B
, which is indeed what we shall show we have.

If those two conditions hold, then the free parameters
x_{2},y_{3},y_{4}
must satisfy the conditions
\beta^{\prime}(x_{2},h_{2})=\beta^{\prime}(x_{2},h)=0
and
\beta(x_{2},y_{3})=\beta(x_{2}+w,y_{4})=0
. If
h_{2}
is y-normal for
\beta^{\prime}
, then the probability that
h
is y-normal for the restriction to the subspace
\{x:\beta^{\prime}(x,h_{1})=0\}
is at least
1-p^{2k-t}
, so the conditional probability that
(h,h_{2})
is y-normal for
\beta^{\prime}
is at least
1-p^{2k-t}
. If it is, then
\beta^{\prime}(x_{2},h)=\beta^{\prime}(x_{2},h_{2})=0
with probability
p^{-2k}
. The probability that
x_{2}
and
x_{2}+w
are x-normal for
\beta
is at least
1-2p^{2k-t}
, and if we condition on
\beta^{\prime}(x_{2},h)=\beta^{\prime}(x_{2},h_{2})=0
it is still at least
1-2p^{4k-t}
. If they are x-normal for
\beta
, then
\beta(x_{2},y_{3})=\beta(x_{2}+w,y_{4})=0
with probability
p^{-2k}
.

All told, therefore, the expectation of
b(h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is zero unless
\beta^{\prime}(x_{1},h_{1})=\beta^{\prime}(x_{3},h_{3})=0
, in which case it is
p^{-4k}\pm 3p^{4k-t}
.

We now prove that this gives us a quasirandom sample. This time we let
\mathcal{Q}
be the set of all 4-arrangements
(P_{4},P_{8})
of width
w
, and we let
\mathcal{Q}^{B}
be the set of all these that live in
B
. And given
(h_{1},h_{3})
we let
\mathcal{Q}^{B}(h_{1},h_{3})
be the set of all of them such that
\beta^{\prime}(x_{1},h_{1})=\beta^{\prime}(x_{3},h_{3})=0
, where the choice of parameters is as above.

If we choose a random 4-arrangement in
\mathcal{Q}
, then the probability that it belongs to
\mathcal{Q}^{B}
is at least
p^{-7k}/2
, by Lemma
6.9
. The probability that
x_{1}
and
x_{3}
are both x-normal for
\beta^{\prime}
is at least
1-2p^{k-t}
, so the probability that they are both x-normal for
\beta^{\prime}
given that
(P_{4},P_{8})\in\mathcal{Q}^{B}
is at least
1-4p^{8k-t}
. (Again, this can be improved, since with a little more effort one can show that
x_{1}
and
x_{3}
are approximately uniformly distributed even conditioned on
(P_{4},P_{8})\in\mathcal{Q}^{B}
, but again the improvement is not important.)

We therefore have the following statement. If
(P_{4},P_{8})
is a random element of
\mathcal{Q}^{B}
, then with probability at least
1-4p^{8k-t}
the density of
(h_{1},h_{3})
such that
\beta^{\prime}(x_{1},h_{1})=\beta^{\prime}(x_{3},h_{3})=0
is
p^{-2k}
. That is, with probability at least
1-4p^{8k-t}
, the density of
(h_{1},h_{3})
such that
(P_{4},P_{8})\in\mathcal{Q}^{B}(h_{1},h_{3})
is
p^{-2k}
.

Similarly, if
(P_{4},P_{8})
and
(P_{4}^{\prime},P_{8}^{\prime})
are two random elements of
\mathcal{Q}^{B}
, then the pairs
(x_{1},x_{1}^{\prime})
and
(x_{3},x_{3}^{\prime})
are x-normal for
\beta^{\prime}
with probability at least
1-8p^{16k-t}
, and if they are normal, then the density of
(h_{1},h_{3})
such that both
(P_{4},P_{8})
and
(P_{4}^{\prime},P_{8}^{\prime})
are in
\mathcal{Q}(h_{1},h_{3})
is
p^{-4k}
.

We now apply Lemma
6.7
with
\alpha=p^{-2k}
,
\epsilon_{1}=4p^{8k-t}
,
\epsilon_{2}=8p^{16k-t}
and
\theta>0
to be chosen. For each
(P_{4},P_{8})\in\mathcal{Q}^{B}
let
f(P_{4},P_{8})=d\bigl(\phi(P_{4}),\phi(P_{8})\bigr)
. Then Lemma
6.7
implies that

\Bigl|\mathbb{E}_{(P_{4},P_{8})\in\mathcal{Q}^{B}}d\bigl(\phi(P_{4}),\phi(P_{8})\bigr)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{Q}^{B}(h_{1},h_{3})}(P_{4},P_{8})-p^{-2k}\mathbb{E}_{(P_{4},P_{8})\in\mathcal{Q}^{B}}d\bigl(\phi(P_{4}),\phi(P_{8})\bigr)\Bigr|\leq\theta

for all
(h_{1},h_{3})
outside a subset of density at most
(8p^{6k-t}+8p^{16k-t})/\theta^{2}
. Since
d\bigl(\phi(P_{4}),\phi(P_{8})\bigr)=0
when
(P_{4},P_{8})\notin\mathcal{Q}^{B}
, and since
\mathcal{Q}^{B}
has density at most
2p^{-7k}
in
\mathcal{Q}
, it follows that

\Bigl|\mathbb{E}_{(P_{4},P_{8})\in\mathcal{Q}}\,d\bigl(\phi(P_{4}),\phi(P_{8})\bigr)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{Q}^{B}(h_{1},h_{3})}(P_{4},P_{8})-p^{-2k}\mathbb{E}_{(P_{4},P_{8})\in\mathcal{Q}}\,d\bigl(\phi(P_{4}),\phi(P_{8})\bigr)\Bigr|\leq 2\theta p^{-7k}.

If
(P_{4},P_{8})\in Q^{B}(h_{1},h_{3})
, then, as we have already pointed out, the expectation of
b(h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
given
(P_{4},P_{8})
is
p^{-4k}\pm 3p^{4k-t}
, and otherwise it is zero, except that if
(h,h_{2})
is not y-normal for
\beta^{\prime}
and
(P_{4},P_{8})\in Q^{B}(h_{1},h_{3})
, then we just know that the expectation is between 0 and 1. The last event happens with probability at most
p^{2k-t}
. It follows that

\displaystyle\Bigl|p^{-4k}\mathbb{E}_{(P_{4},P_{8})\in\mathcal{Q}}d\bigl(\phi(P_{4}),\phi(P_{8})\bigr)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{Q}^{B}(h_{1},h_{3})}(P_{4},P_{8})

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}_{(P_{4},P_{8})\in\mathcal{Q}}d\bigl(\phi(P_{4}),\phi(P_{8})\bigr)\mathbb{E}[b(h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})|(P_{4},P_{8})]\Bigr|\leq 4p^{4k-t}.

The second term is equal to
\mathbb{E}_{h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}b(h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})d\bigl(\phi(P_{4}),\phi(P_{8})\bigr)
, so by the triangle inequality we deduce that

\mathbb{E}_{h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}b(h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})d\bigl(\phi(P_{4}),\phi(P_{8})\bigr)\leq p^{-6k}\mathbb{E}_{(P_{4},P_{8})\in\mathcal{Q}}\,d\bigl(\phi(P_{4}),\phi(P_{8})\bigr)+3p^{9k-t}+2\theta p^{-11k}.

The same argument works for the other two terms (indeed, after suitable changes of variable we can use precisely the same words). Putting everything together then gives us the inequality

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}

\displaystyle d\bigl(\phi(P_{1})\phi(P_{2}),\phi(P_{3})\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq p^{-2k}\mathbb{E}_{h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}\bigl(d\bigl(\phi(P_{4},P_{8})\bigr)+d\bigl(\phi(P_{5},P_{6})\bigr)+d\bigl(\phi(P_{7},P_{9})\bigr)\bigr)+p^{8k-t}+9p^{13k-t}+6\theta p^{-7k}

for all pairs
(h_{1},h_{2})
outside a subset of density at most
(24p^{6k-t}+24p^{16k-t})/\theta^{2}+3p^{k-t}
, which is equivalent to the result claimed.
∎

As we have already noted, the “trivial” upper bound for
\mathbb{E}_{h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}d\bigl(\phi(P_{4}),\phi(P_{8})\bigr)
is
2p^{-7k}
, since this is an upper bound for the probability that a random 4-arrangement of width
w
lies in
B
. Therefore, for the inequality just proved to be of any interest we need to choose
\theta
small compared with
p^{-2k}
.

It remains to bound the expectation of
d\bigl(\phi(P_{1}),\phi(P_{2})\bigr)
over 4-arrangements
(P_{1},P_{2})
of width
w
by the expectation over
all
4-arrangements.

For the next lemma, let
\mathcal{Q}(w)
be the set of all 4-arrangements of width
w
and let
\mathcal{Q}^{B}(w)
be the set of 4-arrangements of width
w
that lie in
B
.

Lemma 6.15
.

Let
\theta>0
. Then for all
w
outside a set of density at most
(4p^{7k-t}+4p^{16k-t})/\theta^{2}
, we have the inequality

\mathbb{E}_{(P_{1},P_{2})\in\mathcal{Q}(w)}d\bigl(\phi(P_{1}),\phi(P_{2})\bigr)\leq 2\mathbb{E}_{(P_{3},P_{5})\in\mathcal{Q}}\,d\bigl(\phi(P_{3}),\phi(P_{5})\bigr)+2p^{-2k}\theta+4p^{k-t}.

Proof.

Let
x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},y_{4}
and
h
be random elements of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}
, and let
P_{1}=P(w,h;x_{1},y_{1},y_{2})
and
P_{2}=P(w,h;x_{2},y_{3},y_{4})
.
Now let
y_{5},y_{6}
and
u
be three more random elements of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}
and define vertical edges
E_{1}=\bigl((x_{1}+u,y_{5}),(x_{1}+u,y_{5}+h)\bigr)
and
E_{2}=\bigl((x_{2}+u,y_{6}),(x_{2}+u,y_{6}+h)\bigr)
. Using
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
and the vertical edges of
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
we can create four more vertical parallelograms, namely
P_{3}=P(u,h;x_{1},y_{1},y_{5})
,
P_{4}=P(w-u,h;x_{1}+u,y_{5},y_{2})
,
P_{5}=P(u,h;x_{2},y_{3},y_{6})
and
P_{6}=P(w-u,h;x_{2}+u,y_{6},y_{4})
.

Write
\mathbf{x}
for
(x_{1},x_{2})
and
\mathbf{y}
for
(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},y_{4},y_{5},y_{6})
. Then for all choices of
h,u,\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
we have that

\displaystyle d\bigl(\phi(P_{1}),\phi(P_{2})\bigr)

\displaystyle=d\bigl(\phi_{L}(P_{1})\phi_{L}(P_{2})^{*},\phi_{R}(P_{1})\phi_{R}(P_{2})^{*}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq d\bigl(\phi_{L}(P_{1})\phi_{L}(P_{2})^{*},\phi(E_{1})\phi(E_{2})^{*}\bigr)+d\bigl(\phi(E_{1})\phi(E_{2})^{*},\phi_{R}(P_{1})\phi_{R}(P_{2})^{*}\bigr)

\displaystyle=d\bigl(\phi_{L}(P_{1})\phi(E_{1})^{*},\phi_{L}(P_{2})\phi(E_{2})^{*}\bigr)+d\bigl(\phi(E_{1})\phi_{R}(P_{1})^{*},\phi(E_{2})\phi_{R}(P_{2})^{*}\bigr)

\displaystyle=d\bigl(\phi(P_{3}),\phi(P_{5})\bigr)+d\bigl(\phi(P_{4}),\phi(P_{6})\bigr).

Note that the 4-arrangements
(P_{3},P_{5})
and
(P_{4},P_{6})
are uniformly distributed amongst all 4-arrangements.

As usual, we now introduce a function that tells us when the entire collection of parallelograms belongs to
B
. Given
u,h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
, let
b(u,h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=1
if all the vertices of all the parallelograms above are in
B
and let
b(u,h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=0
otherwise. Then from the above calculation we deduce that

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{u,h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}b(u,h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})d\bigl(\phi(P_{1}),\phi(P_{2})\bigr)\leq\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{u,h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}b(u,h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\bigl(d\bigl(\phi(P_{3}),\phi(P_{5})\bigr)+d\bigl(\phi(P_{4}),\phi(P_{6})\bigr)\bigr).

Now we estimate the three terms above, beginning with the term on the left-hand side. Fix a 4-arrangement
(P_{1},P_{2})
of width
w
and height
h
. If
h
is y-normal for
\beta^{\prime\prime}
, which it is unless it lies in an exceptional set of density at most
p^{k-t}
, then the proportion of
u
with
\beta^{\prime\prime}(u,h)=0
is
p^{-k}
. If
x_{1}+u
and
x_{2}+u
are x-normal for
\beta
, which they are unless
u
lies in an exceptional set of density at most
2p^{k-t}
, then the proportion of
(y_{5},y_{6})
such that
\beta(x_{1}+u,y_{5})=\beta(x_{2}+u,y_{6})=0
is
p^{-2k}
. And if those equations all hold, then
b(u,h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=1
, since

\displaystyle\beta(x_{1}+u,y_{5}+h)

\displaystyle=\beta(x_{1}+u,y_{6})+\beta^{\prime}(x_{1}+u,h)

\displaystyle=\beta(x_{1}+u,y_{5})+\beta^{\prime}(x_{1},h)+\beta^{\prime\prime}(u,h)

\displaystyle=\beta(x_{1}+u,y_{5})+\beta(x_{1},y_{1}+h)-\beta(x_{1},y_{1})+\beta^{\prime\prime}(u,h)

\displaystyle=0,

and similarly
\beta(x_{2}+u,y_{6}+h)=0
.

It follows that

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{u,h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}b(u,h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})d\bigl(\phi(P_{1}),\phi(P_{2})\bigr)=p^{-3k}\mathbb{E}_{h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}d\bigl(\phi(P_{1}),\phi(P_{2})\bigr)\pm 3p^{k-t}.

Now let us look at the term
\mathbb{E}_{u,h,\mathbf{x}\,y}b(u,h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})d\bigl(\phi(P_{3}),\phi(P_{5})\bigr)
. Once we know
P_{3}
and
P_{5}
the parameters
x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{3},y_{5},y_{6},u
and
h
are determined, and it remains to choose
y_{2}
and
y_{4}
. If we know that
(P_{3},P_{4})
is in
B
, then for
b(u,h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
to equal 1 we need

\beta(x_{1}+w,y_{2})=\beta(x_{1}+w,y_{2}+h)=\beta(x_{2}+w,y_{4})=\beta(x_{2}+w,y_{4}+h)=0.

This is possible only if
\beta^{\prime}(x_{1}+w,h)=\beta^{\prime}(x_{2}+w,h)=0
. Since we already know that
\beta^{\prime}(x_{1},h)=\beta^{\prime}(x_{2},h)=0
(from the fact that
(P_{3},P_{5})
is in
B
), it follows that the one requirement that must be satisfied is that
\beta^{\prime\prime}(w,h)=0
. Since this is not satisfied for all 4-arrangements
(P_{3},P_{5})
, we must again argue that we have a quasirandom sample.

First, let us assume that
\beta^{\prime\prime}(w,h)=0
and estimate the expected value of
\beta(u,h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
given
(P_{3},P_{5})
. If
x_{1}+w
and
x_{2}+w
are x-normal for
\beta
, then the density of
(y_{2},y_{4})
such that
\beta(x_{1}+w,y_{2})=\beta(x_{2}+w,y_{4})=0
is
p^{-2k}
. And if those equations hold, then we also have the equations
\beta(x_{1}+w,y_{2}+h)=\beta(x_{2}+w,y_{4}+h)=0
, since
\beta^{\prime}(x_{1},h)=\beta^{\prime}(x_{2},h)=0
and we are assuming that
\beta^{\prime\prime}(w,h)=0
. The density of
(x_{1},x_{2})
such that
x_{1}+w
and
x_{2}+w
are x-normal for
\beta
is at least
1-2p^{k-t}
, so this implies that the expected value is
p^{-2k}\pm 2p^{k-t}
.

Now we show the quasirandomness. Let us define
\mathcal{Q}[w]
to be the set of 4-arrangements of height
h
with
\beta^{\prime\prime}(w,h)=0
and
\mathcal{Q}^{B}[w]
to be the set of all those that lie in
B
. Given a 4-arrangement
(P_{3},P_{5})\in\mathcal{Q}^{B}
of height
h
, if
h
is y-normal for
\beta^{\prime\prime}
, then the proportion of
w
with
(P_{3},P_{5})\in\mathcal{Q}^{B}[w]
is
p^{-k}
. Given two 4-arrangements
(P_{3},P_{5})
and
(P_{3}^{\prime},P_{5}^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{Q}^{B}
of heights
h
and
h^{\prime}
, if
(h,h^{\prime})
is y-normal for
\beta^{\prime\prime}
, then the proportion of
w
such that both
(P_{3},P_{5})
and
(P_{3}^{\prime},P_{5}^{\prime})
are in
\mathcal{Q}^{B}[w]
is
p^{-2k}
. We may therefore apply Lemma
6.7
with
\alpha=p^{-k}
, the
A_{i}
being the sets
\mathcal{Q}^{B}[w]
,
X
being
\mathcal{Q}^{B}
,
\epsilon_{1}=2p^{8k-t}
, and
\epsilon_{2}=2p^{16k-t}
. (Again, these choices of
\epsilon_{1}
and
\epsilon_{2}
are inefficient: we are ignoring the fact that the height is roughly uniformly distributed when we choose a random
(P_{3},P_{5})\in\mathcal{Q}^{B}
.) The lemma gives us that for every
\theta>0
we have the inequality

\Bigl|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{(P_{3},P_{5})\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{Q}^{B}[w]}(P_{3},P_{5})d\bigl(\phi(P_{3}),\phi(P_{5})\bigr)-p^{-k}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{(P_{3},P_{5})\in\mathcal{Q}}d\bigl(\phi(P_{3}),\phi(P_{5})\bigr)\Bigr|\leq\theta

as long as
w
lies outside a set of at most
(2p^{7k-t}+2p^{16k-t})/\theta^{2}
. But the estimate in the previous paragraph also gives us that

\Bigl|p^{-2k}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{(P_{3},P_{5})\in\mathcal{Q}}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{Q}^{B}[w]}(P_{3},P_{5})d\bigl(\phi(P_{3}),\phi(P_{5})\bigr)-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{u,h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}b(u,h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})d\bigl(\phi(P_{3}),\phi(P_{5})\bigr)\Bigr|\leq 2p^{k-t}.

Therefore, by the triangle inequality we have

\Bigl|\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{u,h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}b(u,h,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})d\bigl(\phi(P_{3}),\phi(P_{5})\bigr)-p^{-3k}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{(P_{3},P_{5})\in\mathcal{Q}}d\bigl(\phi(P_{3}),\phi(P_{5})\bigr)\Bigr|\leq p^{-2k}\theta+2p^{k-t}

as long as
w
lies outside a set of at most
(2p^{7k-t}+2p^{16k-t})/\theta^{2}
. (It is not obvious from the notation, but the dependence of the above statement on
w
is via the function
b
, which we defined for a fixed
w
but which is a different function for different values of
w
.)

The same argument applies to the term with
P_{4}
and
P_{6}
, which gives the same answer because both
(P_{3},P_{5})
and
(P_{4},P_{6})
are uniformly distributed over all 4-arrangements. This proves the lemma.
∎

6.5.
Approximating
\psi
by a single-valued function with good bilinearity properties

In this subsection, we put together the results of the previous two subsections and find a function that is close to
\psi
, linear in each direction separately, and defined on almost all of
B
.

First, let us see what we now know about
\psi
, if we assume that
\phi
is a
(1-\eta)
-bihomomorphism with respect to the characteristic function
b
of
B
. Recall that this is equivalent to saying that

\mathbb{E}_{(P_{1},P_{2})\in\mathcal{Q}}d\bigl(\phi(P_{1}),\phi(P_{2})\bigr)\leq\eta\mathbb{E}_{(P_{1},P_{2})\in\mathcal{Q}}b(P_{1},P_{2}),

where as usual
b(P_{1},P_{2})
is shorthand for the product of the values of
b
over all the points in
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
(so it is 1 if the 4-arrangement
(P_{1},P_{2})
lies in
B
and 0 otherwise) and
\mathcal{Q}
is the set of all 4-arrangements in the whole of
(\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n})^{2}
. Recall also that the left-hand side above is equal to
\mathbb{E}_{w^{\prime},h^{\prime}}d\bigl(\psi(w^{\prime},h^{\prime}),\psi(w^{\prime},h^{\prime})\bigr)
.

Lemma
6.12
gives us that

d\bigl(\psi(w_{1},h)\psi(w_{2},h),\psi(w_{1}+w_{2},h)\bigr)\leq 2p^{-2k}\mathbb{E}_{w}d\bigl(\psi(w,h),\psi(w,h)\bigr)\pm 7p^{2k-t}

for all
w_{1},w_{2}
and for all
h
outside a set of density at most
p^{k-t}
such that
\beta^{\prime\prime}(w_{1},h)=\beta^{\prime\prime}(w_{2},h)=0
, those two equations being a trivial necessary condition for the left-hand side of the inequality not to be zero. Then Lemma
6.13
and our hypothesis tell us that for every
\theta>0
we have the inequality

\mathbb{E}_{w}d\bigl(\psi(w,h),\psi(w,h)\bigr)\leq 2\eta\mathbb{E}_{(P_{1},P_{2})\in\mathcal{Q}}b(P_{1},P_{2})+2\theta

for all
h
outside a set of density at most
12p^{26k-t}/\theta^{2}
.

Corollary
6.9
tells us (provided that
t\geq 7k
) that
\mathbb{E}_{(P_{1},P_{2})\in\mathcal{Q}}b(P_{1},P_{2})=p^{-7k}\pm 6p^{-t}
, so the above inequality implies that

\mathbb{E}_{w}d\bigl(\psi(w,h),\psi(w,h)\bigr)\leq 2\eta p^{-7k}+12p^{-t}+2\theta.

Plugging this bound into the first inequality gives us the inequality

d\bigl(\psi(w_{1},h)\psi(w_{2},h),\psi(w_{1}+w_{2},h)\bigr)\leq 4\eta p^{-9k}+24p^{-2k-t}+2\theta p^{-2k}+7p^{2k-t}.

Therefore, if
\theta\leq\eta p^{-7k}
and
t\geq 12k+\log_{p}(7\eta^{-1})
, we obtain the inequality

d\bigl(\psi(w_{1},h)\psi(w_{2},h),\psi(w_{1}+w_{2},h)\bigr)\leq 8\eta p^{-9k}.

Recall that this holds for all
h
outside a set of density at most
p^{k-t}+12p^{26k-t}/\theta^{2}
.

The main point here is that we can choose some
\theta\leq\eta p^{-7k}
, and having made that choice we can choose
t
such that the exceptional set of
h
has some small density that can depend on
k
. For the time being we shall defer making an actual choice, but we note that the condition on
t
will be that it is bounded below by some multiple of
k
(by an absolute constant).

Now let us obtain a similar inequality that demonstrates linearity in the second variable for
\psi
. When
w
is x-normal for
\beta^{\prime}
, Lemma
6.14
gives us that

d\bigl(\psi(w,h_{1})\psi(w,h_{2}),\psi(w,h_{1}+h_{2})\bigr)\leq 2p^{-2k}\mathbb{E}_{h}d(\psi(w,h),\psi(w,h))+10p^{13k-t}+6\theta p^{-7k}

for all pairs
(h_{1},h_{2})
outside a set of density at most
25p^{16k-t}/\theta^{2}
. Now for each
w
, if we expand out
\psi(w,h)
and apply Lemma
6.15
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{h}d(\psi(w,h),\psi(w,h))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{(P_{1},P_{2})\in\mathcal{Q}(w)}d\bigl(\phi(P_{1}),\phi(P_{2})\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}_{(P_{3},P_{5})\in\mathcal{Q}}\,d\bigl(\phi(P_{3}),\phi(P_{5})\bigr)+2p^{-2k}\theta+4p^{k-t}

provided that
w
lies outside a set of density at most
(4p^{7k-t}+4p^{16k-t})/\theta^{2}
. Therefore, for the non-exceptional triples
(w,h_{1},h_{2})
, we have

\displaystyle d\bigl(\psi(w,h_{1})\psi(w,h_{2}),

\displaystyle\psi(w,h_{1}+h_{2})\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq 4p^{-2k}\mathbb{E}_{(P_{3},P_{5})\in\mathcal{Q}}\,d\bigl(\phi(P_{3}),\phi(P_{5})\bigr)+4p^{-4k}\theta+8p^{-k-t}+10p^{13k-t}+6\theta p^{-7k}

\displaystyle\leq 4p^{-2k}(\eta(p^{-7k}+6p^{-t})+5p^{-4k}\theta+11p^{13k-t}

\displaystyle\leq 4\eta p^{-9k}+5p^{-4k}\theta+12p^{13k-t}.

If we choose
\theta
to be at most
\eta p^{-5k}/5
, then we can choose
t
in such a way that

d\bigl(\psi(w,h_{1})\psi(w,h_{2}),\psi(w,h_{1}+h_{2})\bigr)\leq 6\eta p^{-9k}

for all
w
and for all
(h_{1},h_{2})
outside exceptional sets of densities that can depend on
k
.

Let us summarize this information in the form of a lemma.

Lemma 6.16
.

Let
\delta>0
and suppose that
t\geq 44k+\log(\eta^{-1})+\log(\delta^{-1})
. Then

d\bigl(\psi(w_{1},h)\psi(w_{2},h),\psi(w_{1}+w_{2},h)\bigr)\leq 8\eta p^{-9k}

for all triples
(w_{1},w_{2},h)
outside a set of density at most
p^{-8k}/2500
, and

d\bigl(\psi(w,h_{1})\psi(w,h_{2}),\psi(w,h_{1}+h_{2})\bigr)\leq 8\eta p^{-9k}

for all triples
(w,h_{1},h_{2})
outside a set of density at most
p^{-8k}/2500
.

Proof.

Let us set
\theta=\eta p^{-7k}/5
. Then the first inequality holds outside a set of density at most
20p^{26k-t}/\theta^{2}=500p^{40k-t}/\eta^{2}
, and the second holds outside a set of density at most
25p^{16k-t}/\theta^{2}=625p^{30k-t}/\eta^{2}
. These densities are both at most
\delta
when
t\geq 44k+\log(\eta^{-1})+\log(\delta^{-1})
. (Here we used the fact that
p\geq 5
, so
p^{4}\geq 625
.)
∎

In order to use Lemma
6.16
to obtain a single-valued function, we prove a few more easy facts about our distance function.

Lemma 6.17
.

Let
f,g
be non-negative functions in
\mathcal{A}
and suppose that
d(f,g)\leq\theta\|f\|_{1}\|g\|_{1}
. Then
d(f,f)\leq 2\theta\|f\|_{1}^{2}
and
d(g,g)\leq 2\theta\|g\|_{1}^{2}
.

Proof.

From the assumption and the bilinearity of
d
, we obtain the inequality

d(\|g\|_{1}f,\|f\|_{1},g)\leq\theta\|f\|_{1}^{2}\|g\|_{1}^{2}.

It follows from the symmetry of
d
and the triangle inequality that

d(\|g\|_{1}f,\|g\|_{1}f)\leq 2\theta\|f\|_{1}^{2}\|g\|_{1}^{2},

and then by bilinearity again that

d(f,f)\leq 2\theta\|f\|_{1}^{2}.

By symmetry we have the inequality for
g
as well.
∎

Lemma 6.18
.

Let
f
be a non-negative function in
\mathcal{A}
, let
0\leq\theta<1/2
and suppose that
d(f,f)\leq\theta\|f\|_{1}^{2}
. Then there exists a unique
x
such that
f(x)\geq(1-\theta)\|f\|_{1}
.

Proof.

The hypothesis implies that
\langle f,f\rangle\geq(1-\theta)\|f\|_{1}^{2}
. But we also know that
\langle f,f\rangle\geq\|f\|_{1}\|f\|_{\infty}
. It follows that
\|f\|_{\infty}\geq(1-\theta)\|f\|_{1}
, which proves the lemma (the uniqueness being obvious).
∎

Corollary 6.19
.

Let
0\leq\theta<1/25
and let
f,g,h
be three non-negative functions in
\mathcal{A}
and suppose that
d(fg,h)\leq\theta\|f\|_{1}\|g\|_{1}\|h\|_{1}
. Then there exist unique
x,y,z\in G
such that
f(x)\geq(1-2\theta)\|f\|_{1}
,
g(y)\geq(1-2\theta)\|g\|_{1}
,
h(z)\geq(1-2\theta)\|h\|_{1}
, and
x+y=z
.

Proof.

Since
\|fg\|_{1}=\|f\|_{1}\|g\|_{1}
for any two non-negative functions
f,g\in\mathcal{A}
, and since
\langle fg,h\rangle=\langle f,g^{*}h\rangle
, Lemma
6.17
implies that
d(f,f)\leq 2\theta\|f\|_{1}^{2}
,
d(g,g)\leq 2\theta\|g\|_{1}^{2}
and
d(h,h)\leq 2\theta\|h\|_{1}^{2}
. The existence and uniqueness of
x,y,z
satisfying the three inequalities now follows from Lemma
6.18
.

Write
\tilde{f}
for the function that takes the value
\|f\|_{1}
at
x
and 0 elsewhere, and define
\tilde{g}
and
\tilde{h}
similarly. Then
\|f-\tilde{f}\|_{1}\leq 4\theta\|f\|_{1}
, with corresponding estimates for
g
and
h
. From this it follows that

d(\tilde{f}\tilde{g},\tilde{h})=d(\tilde{f}\tilde{g},\tilde{h}-h)+d(\tilde{f}\tilde{g},h)\leq 4\theta\|f\|_{1}\|g\|_{1}\|h\|_{1}+d(\tilde{f}\tilde{g},h),

and then that

d(\tilde{f}\tilde{g},h)=d(\tilde{g},\tilde{f}^{*}h)=d(\tilde{g}-g,\tilde{f}^{*}h)+d(g,\tilde{f}^{*}h)\leq 4\theta\|f\|_{1}\|g\|_{1}\|h\|_{1}+d(g,\tilde{f}^{*}h),

and thirdly that

d(g,\tilde{f}^{*}h)=d(\tilde{f},g^{*}h)=d(\tilde{f}-f,g^{*}h)+d(\tilde{f},g^{*}h)\leq 4\theta\|f\|_{1}\|g\|_{1}\|h\|_{1}+d(f,g^{*}h).

It follows that
d(\tilde{f}\tilde{g},\tilde{h})\leq 25\theta\|\tilde{f}\|_{1}\|\tilde{g}\|_{1}\|\tilde{h}\|_{1}
, and therefore that
\langle\tilde{f}\tilde{g},\tilde{h}\rangle>0
, which can happen only if
x+y=z
, since
\tilde{f},\tilde{g}
and
\tilde{h}
are multiples of delta functions.
∎

Lemma 6.20
.

For all
(w,h)\in B^{\prime\prime}
outside a set of density at most
p^{k-t}
, the probability that a random vertical parallelogram of width
w
and height
h
lives in
B
is
p^{-3k}\pm 2p^{k-t}
.

Proof.

Every
h
outside a set of density at most
p^{k-t}
is y-normal for
\beta^{\prime}
. For each such
h
, the density of
x
such that
\beta^{\prime}(x,h)=0
is
p^{-k}
. We are assuming that
\beta^{\prime\prime}(w,h)=0
. Therefore, if
\beta^{\prime}(x,h)=0
, then
\beta^{\prime}(x+w,h)=0
as well. The density of
x
such that
x
and
x+w
are x-normal for
\beta
is at least
1-2p^{k-t}
, and if they are both x-normal for
\beta
, then the density of pairs
(y,y^{\prime})
such that
\beta(x,y)=\beta(x+w,y^{\prime})=0
is
p^{-2k}
. It follows that the probability that
(x,y),(x,y+h),(x+w,y^{\prime})
and
(x+w,y^{\prime}+h)
all lie in
B
is
p^{-3k}\pm 2p^{k-t}
, as claimed.
∎

Using these results, we can now approximate
\psi
by a single-valued function that is very close to being additive in each variable separately.

Lemma 6.21
.

If
\eta<1/400
and
t\geq 44k+\log(\eta^{-1})+\log(\delta^{-1})
, then there is a function
\tilde{\psi}:B\to G
with the following properties.

(1)

\tilde{\psi}(w_{1},h)+\tilde{\psi}(w_{2},h)=\tilde{\psi}(w_{1}+w_{2},h)
for all triples
(w_{1},w_{2},h)
outside a set of density at most
\delta
.

(2)

\tilde{\psi}(w,h_{1})+\tilde{\psi}(w,h_{2})=\tilde{\psi}(w,h_{1}+h_{2})
for all triples
(w,h_{1},h_{2})
outside a set of density at most
\delta
.

(3)

d\bigl(\psi(w,h),\delta_{\tilde{\psi}(w,h)}\bigr)\leq 64\eta p^{-3k}
for all
(w,h)
outside a set of density at most
\delta
.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.16
,
d\bigl(\psi(w_{1},h)\psi(w_{2},h),\psi(w_{1}+w_{2},h)\bigr)\leq 8\eta p^{-9k}

for all triples
(w_{1},w_{2},h)
outside a set of density at most
\delta
. For each such triple, apply Corollary
6.19
to the elements
\psi(w_{1},h),\psi(w_{2},h)
and
\psi(w_{1}+w_{2},h)
of
\mathcal{A}
and let
\tilde{\psi}(w_{1},h),\tilde{\psi}(w_{2},h)
and
\tilde{\psi}(w_{1}+w_{2},h)
be the elements of
G
that it gives.

By Lemma
6.20
,
\|\psi(w,h)\|_{1}=p^{-3k}\pm 2p^{k-t}
for all
(w,h)
outside a set of density at most
p^{k-t}\leq\delta
, so except in a set of triples
(w_{1},w_{2},h)
of density at most
\delta
we can also say that
\|\psi(w_{1},h)\|_{1},\|\psi(w_{2},h)\|_{1}
and
\|\psi(w_{1}+w_{2},h)\|_{1}
are all at least
9p^{-3k}/10
, which implies that

d\bigl(\psi(w_{1},h)\psi(w_{2},h),\psi(w_{1}+w_{2},h)\bigr)\leq 16\eta\|\psi(w_{1},h)\|_{1}\|\psi(w_{2},h)\|_{1}\|\psi(w_{1}+w_{2},h)\|_{1}

outside a set of triples
(w_{1},w_{2},h)
of density at most
\delta
. By Lemma
6.19
, for each such triple we have that
\tilde{\psi}(w_{1},h)+\tilde{\psi}(w_{2},h)=\tilde{\psi}(w_{1}+w_{2},h)
, and the first statement is proved.

The second statement follows by interchanging the roles of the two coordinates and using the symmetry of Lemma
6.16
.

As for the third, each
(w,h)\in B^{\prime\prime}
outside a set of density at most
\delta
is
(w_{1}+w_{2},h)
for some non-exceptional triple
(w_{1},w_{2},h)
, and therefore by Corollary
6.19
satisfies the inequality

\psi(w,h)(\tilde{\psi}(w,h))\geq(1-32\eta)\|\psi(w,h)\|_{1},

which implies the statement.
∎

6.6.
Approximating
\tilde{\psi}
by a function defined on all of
B

We have just obtained a function
\tilde{\psi}:B^{\prime\prime}\to G
that is “almost always additive” in each variable separately. We now wish to approximate
\tilde{\psi}
by a function defined on
B^{\prime\prime}
that is additive in each variable separately.

We begin by restricting
\tilde{\psi}
to a function that is additive in each variable separately and still defined on almost all of
B^{\prime\prime}
. We shall then extend that to a function that is defined on all of
B^{\prime\prime}
.

By the first statement of Lemma
6.21
it follows that for all
h
outside a set of density at most
\delta^{1/2}
we have that
\tilde{\psi}(w_{1},h)+\tilde{\psi}(w_{2},h)=\tilde{\psi}(w_{1}+w_{2},h)
for all pairs
(w_{1},w_{2})
outside a set of density at most
\delta^{1/2}
. In order to exploit this, we shall use the following variants of Lemma
6.6
.

Lemma 6.22
.

Let
0\leq\gamma<1/8
, let
G
be a finite Abelian group, and let
A\subset G
and
\phi:G\to G
be such that for a fraction at least
1-\gamma
of pairs
(x,y)\in G^{2}
we have that
x,y,x+y\in A
and
\phi(x+y)=\phi(x)+\phi(y)
. Then there is a subset
A_{1}\subset A
of density at least
1-4\gamma
in
G
such that
\phi(x+y)=\phi(x)+\phi(y)
whenever
x,y,x+y\in A_{1}
.

Proof.

For all
z
outside a set
B
of density at most
1-4\gamma
it is the case that for all
x
outside a set of density at most 1/4 the equation
\phi(z)=\phi(x)+\phi(z-x)
holds (and in particular both sides are defined).

If
z_{1},z_{2}\in B
, then choose
x_{1},x_{2}
at random. Then each of the equations
\phi(z_{1})=\phi(x_{1})+\phi(z_{1}-x_{1})
,
\phi(z_{2})=\phi(x_{2})+\phi(z_{2}-x_{2})
and
\phi(z_{1}+z_{2})=\phi(x_{1}+x_{2})+\phi(z_{1}+z_{2}-x_{1}-x_{2})
holds with probability at least 3/4, so with probability at least 1/4 all three equations hold.

We also have each of the equations
\phi(x_{1}+x_{2})=\phi(x_{1})+\phi(x_{2})
and
\phi(z_{1}+z_{2}-x_{1}-x_{2})=\phi(z_{1}-x_{1})+\phi(z_{2}-x_{2})
with probability at least
1-\gamma
, so with non-zero probability all five equations hold, which implies that
\phi(z_{1}+z_{2})=\phi(z_{1})+\phi(z_{2})
.
∎

Lemma 6.23
.

Let
0\leq\theta<1/6
, let
G
be a finite Abelian group, let
A_{1}
be a subset of
G
of density at least
1-\theta
, and let
\phi:A_{1}\to G
be additive. Then
\phi
has a unique extension to an additive function
\phi_{1}
defined on all of
G
.

Proof.

For each
z\in G
there exist
x,y\in A_{1}
such that
x+y=z
. Therefore if an extension exists, the value of
\phi_{1}(z)
has to be
\phi(x)+\phi(y)
.

We now prove that this gives a well-defined function. To do this, suppose that
x+y=x^{\prime}+y^{\prime}
. Since
\theta<1/6
, we can find
d
such that all of
x+d,y+d,x^{\prime}+d,y^{\prime}+d
and
x+y+d=x^{\prime}+y^{\prime}+d
and
x+y+2d=x^{\prime}+y^{\prime}+2d
belong to
A_{1}
. From this and our hypothesis we obtain the three equations

\phi(x)+\phi(y+d)=\phi(x^{\prime})+\phi(y^{\prime}+d),

\phi(x+d)+\phi(y)=\phi(x^{\prime}+d)+\phi(y^{\prime})

and

\phi(x+d)+\phi(y+d)=\phi(x^{\prime}+d)+\phi(y^{\prime}+d).

Subtracting the last from the sum of the first two gives us that
\phi(x)+\phi(y)=\phi(x^{\prime})+\phi(y^{\prime})
, which establishes that the extension is well-defined.

A similar argument shows that it is additive. Indeed, let
z_{1},z_{2}\in G
, pick
x_{1},x_{2}
at random, and let
y_{1}=z_{1}-x_{1}
and
y_{2}=z_{2}-x_{2}
. Then with probability at least
1-6\theta>0
all of
x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2},x_{1}+x_{2}
and
y_{1}+y_{2}
belong to
A
, and if they do, then

\displaystyle\phi_{1}(z_{1}+z_{2})

\displaystyle=\phi(x_{1}+x_{2})+\phi(y_{1}+y_{2})

\displaystyle=\phi(x_{1})+\phi(x_{2})+\phi(y_{1})+\phi(y_{2})

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}(x_{1}+y_{1})+\phi_{1}(x_{2}+y_{2})

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}(z_{1})+\phi_{1}(z_{2}).

This completes the proof.
∎

It follows that for all
h
outside a set of density at most
\delta^{1/2}
we can remove at most
4\delta^{1/2}|G|
points
(w,h)
from
B^{\prime\prime}_{\bullet h}
in such a way that
\tilde{\psi}(w_{1},h)+\tilde{\psi}(w_{2},h)=\tilde{\psi}(w_{1}+w_{2},h)
whenever
(w_{1},h),(w_{2},h)
and
(w_{1}+w_{2},h)
belong to
B^{\prime\prime}
and have not been removed. And from that it follows that
B^{\prime\prime}
has a subset
B_{1}
such that the restriction of
\tilde{\psi}
to
B_{1}
is additive in the first variable, and such that
B^{\prime\prime}\setminus B_{1}
has density at most
5\delta^{1/2}
in
G
.

Similarly,
B^{\prime\prime}
has a subset
B_{2}
with the same properties but with the roles of the coordinates exchanged. Now let
B_{3}=B_{1}\cap B_{2}
. Then
B^{\prime\prime}\setminus B_{3}
has density at most
10\delta^{1/2}
, and the restriction of
\tilde{\psi}
to
B_{3}
is additive in each variable separately.

The density of
(B^{\prime\prime}\setminus B_{3})_{\bullet h}
is at most
4\delta^{1/4}
for all
h
outside a set of density at most
4\delta^{1/4}
, and the density of
(B^{\prime\prime}\setminus B_{3})_{w\bullet}
is at most
4\delta^{1/4}
for all
w
outside a set of density at most
4\delta^{1/4}
. Therefore, we can find a subset
B_{4}\subset B_{3}
with the following properties.

(1)

For every
h
, either
(B_{4})_{\bullet h}=\emptyset
or
(B^{\prime\prime}\setminus B_{4})_{\bullet h}
has density at most
8\delta^{1/4}
, and the density of
h
for which
(B_{4})_{\bullet h}=\emptyset
is at most
4\delta^{1/4}
.

(2)

For every
w
, either
(B_{4})_{w\bullet}=\emptyset
or
(B^{\prime\prime}\setminus B_{4})_{w\bullet}
has density at most
8\delta^{1/4}
, and the density of
w
for which
(B_{4})_{w\bullet}=\emptyset
is at most
4\delta^{1/4}
.

(3)

The density of
B^{\prime\prime}\setminus B_{4}
is at most
8\delta^{1/4}
.

The set
B_{4}
also inherits from
B_{3}
the property that the restriction of
\tilde{\psi}
is additive in each variable separately.

Lemma 6.24
.

Assume that
8\delta^{1/4}<p^{-2k}/6
. Then
\tilde{\psi}
can be extended to a function
\tilde{\psi}_{2}
that is defined on all of
B^{\prime\prime}
and is still additive in each variable separately.

Proof.

Let
H
be the set of all
h
such that
(B_{4})_{\bullet h}
is non-empty, and let
\zeta=8\delta^{1/4}
. Since each set
B^{\prime\prime}_{\bullet h}
is a subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}
of codimension at most
k
, and therefore density at least
p^{-k}
, our bound for
\zeta
, together with property (1) above, implies that the relative density of
(B_{4})_{\bullet h}
in
B^{\prime\prime}_{\bullet h}
is greater than
5/6
, and therefore Lemma
6.23
implies that
\tilde{\psi}_{\bullet h}
can be extended to an additive function defined on all of
B^{\prime\prime}_{\bullet h}
. Putting all these functions together gives a function
\tilde{\psi}_{1}
that is additive in the first variable and is defined on all of the set
\{(w,h)\in B^{\prime\prime}:h\in H\}
.

We now check that
\tilde{\psi}_{1}
is still additive in the second variable. This is where it is crucial that we were able to ensure that our error parameter
\zeta
was small compared with
p^{-k}
. Let
w\in G
and let
h_{1},h_{2}\in H
be such that
\beta^{\prime\prime}(w,h_{1})=\beta^{\prime\prime}(w,h_{2})=0
, which implies that
\beta^{\prime\prime}(w,h_{1}+h_{2})=0
. The set
B^{\prime\prime}_{\bullet h_{1}}\cap B^{\prime\prime}_{\bullet h_{2}}
is a subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}
of codimension at most
2k
and therefore density at least
p^{-2k}
, and it is contained in the subspace
B^{\prime\prime}_{\bullet(h_{1}+h_{2})}
. By our bound on
\zeta
and the fact that
(B^{\prime\prime}\setminus B_{4})_{\bullet h}
has density at most
\zeta
for each
h\in H
, if we choose a random
w_{1}\in B^{\prime\prime}_{\bullet h_{1}}\cap B^{\prime\prime}_{\bullet h_{2}}
and set
w_{2}=w-w_{1}
, then with non-zero probability the points
(w_{1},h_{1}),(w_{2},h_{1}),(w_{1},h_{2}),(w_{2},h_{2}),(w_{1},h_{1}+h_{2})
and
(w_{2},h_{1}+h_{2})
all belong to
B_{4}
. That gives us the equations

\tilde{\psi}(w_{1},h_{1})+\tilde{\psi}(w_{2},h_{1})=\tilde{\psi}_{1}(w,h_{1}),

\tilde{\psi}(w_{1},h_{2})+\tilde{\psi}(w_{2},h_{2})=\tilde{\psi}_{1}(w,h_{2}),

\tilde{\psi}(w_{1},h_{1}+h_{2})+\tilde{\psi}(w_{2},h_{1}+h_{2})=\tilde{\psi}_{1}(w,h_{1}+h_{2}),

\tilde{\psi}(w_{1},h_{1})+\tilde{\psi}(w_{1},h_{2})=\tilde{\psi}(w_{1},h_{1}+h_{2}),

and

\tilde{\psi}(w_{2},h_{1})+\tilde{\psi}(w_{2},h_{2})=\tilde{\psi}(w_{2},h_{1}+h_{2}).

Together these imply that
\tilde{\psi}_{1}(w,h_{1})+\tilde{\psi}_{1}(w,h_{2})=\tilde{\psi}_{1}(w,h_{1}+h_{2})
, as required.

Let
B_{5}=\{(w,h)\in B^{\prime\prime}:h\in H\}
. Then
B_{5}
has all the properties enjoyed by
B_{4}
, so we can run the same argument with the roles of the two variables interchanged. However, since
H
has density at least
1-\zeta
, if we define
W
to be the set of all
w
such that
(B^{\prime\prime}\setminus B_{5})_{w\bullet}
has density at most
\zeta
, we find that
W
is the whole of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}
. Therefore, at the end of the process we obtain an extension
\tilde{\psi}_{2}
of
\tilde{\psi}_{1}
to the whole of
B^{\prime\prime}
that is additive in each variable separately.
∎

We make one further observation, which we shall not need, but which is of some mild interest. Given a bilinear Bohr set
B^{\prime\prime}
defined by a genuinely bilinear map
\beta^{\prime\prime}
, as opposed to merely a bi-affine map, and given a function
\tilde{\psi}_{2}:B^{\prime\prime}\to G
that is linear in each variable separately, it also respects all 4-arrangements in
B^{\prime\prime}
. Indeed, we have that

\tilde{\psi}_{2}(w,h)-\tilde{\psi}_{2}(w,h+k)-\tilde{\psi}_{2}(w+u,h^{\prime})+\tilde{\psi}_{2}(w+u,h^{\prime}+k)=\tilde{\psi}_{2}(w+u,k)-\tilde{\psi}_{2}(w,k)=\tilde{\psi}_{2}(u,k),

so if
P
is a vertical parallelogram in
B^{\prime\prime}
, then
\tilde{\psi}_{2}(P)
depends only on the width and height of
P
.

6.7.
Putting everything together

We now collect together the results of this section into a single theorem.

Theorem 6.25
.

Let
0\leq\eta<1/400
and let
k
and
t
be such that
t\geq 60k+9\log(\eta^{-1})
. Let
B
be a bilinear Bohr set of codimension
k
and rank
t
, let
\mathcal{A}
be the group algebra of
G
, and let
\phi:G^{2}\to\mathcal{A}
be a function such that
\phi(x,y)\in\mathcal{A}
if
(x,y)\in B
and
\phi(x,y)=0
otherwise. Let
\psi=\mathop{\talloblong\!}\phi
be the mixed convolution of
\phi
. Suppose that
\phi
is a
(1-\eta)
-bihomomorphism. Then there exists a function
\tilde{\psi}_{2}:B^{\prime\prime}\to G
that is additive in each variable separately such that, setting
\psi_{2}(w,h)=\delta_{\tilde{\psi}_{2}(w,h)}
when
(w,h)\in B^{\prime\prime}
and 0 otherwise, we have the inequality

\mathbb{E}_{w,h}d\bigl(\psi(w,h),\psi_{2}(w,h)\bigr)\leq 128\eta p^{-4k}.

Proof.

Note first that by Lemma
6.20
we have that
\|\psi(w,h)\|_{1}\approx p^{-3k}
for almost every
(w,h)\in B^{\prime\prime}
. We also know that
B^{\prime\prime}
itself has density approximately
p^{-k}
and that
\|\psi_{2}\|_{1}=1
for
(w,h)\in B^{\prime\prime}
. Thus,
p^{-4k}
is the trivial upper bound for the problem, and the theorem says that we can beat it by a constant factor that depends on
\eta
, as long as the rank of
B
is sufficiently large.

Now let us set
\delta=16\eta^{4}p^{-16k}
and note that our choice of
t
gives us the required inequality
t\geq 44k+\log(\eta^{-1})+\log(\delta^{-1})
.

To prove the result, we first use Lemma
6.21
to obtain a function
\tilde{\psi}
with the properties stated there. Then the discussion just before Lemma
6.24
gives us a set
B_{4}
such that
B^{\prime\prime}\setminus B_{4}
has density at most
8\delta^{1/4}
and the restriction of
\tilde{\psi}
to
B_{4}
is additive in each variable separately. Lemma
6.24
tells us that this restriction of
\tilde{\psi}
can be extended to a function
\tilde{\psi}_{2}
that is defined on all of
B^{\prime\prime}
and is additive in each variable separately.

The functions
\tilde{\psi}
and
\tilde{\psi}_{2}
agree outside a set of density at most
16\delta^{1/4}
, and statement (3) of Lemma
6.21
tells us that
d\bigl(\psi(w,h),\delta_{\tilde{\psi}(w,h)}\bigr)\leq 64\eta p^{-3k}
for all
(w,h)
outside a set of density at most
\delta
. It follows that
d\bigl(\psi(w,h),\delta_{\tilde{\psi}_{2}(w,h)}\bigr)\leq 64\eta p^{-3k}
for all
(w,h)
outside a set of density at most
17\delta^{1/4}
. Since
B^{\prime\prime}
has density at most
3p^{-k}/2
and
17\delta^{1/4}\leq 32\eta p^{-4k}
, the result follows.
∎

7.
Extending a bihomomorphism from a bilinear Bohr set to the whole of
G^{2}

Let
G=\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}
and let
H
be a finite Abelian group (usually equal to
G
). In this section we shall prove that if
B
is a high-rank bilinear Bohr set and
\phi:B\to H
is affine in each variable separately, then
\phi
can be extended to
G^{2}
while retaining that property. Note that
\phi
here is not the same as the function
\phi
from earlier: it corresponds more closely to the function
\tilde{\psi}_{2}
, but this section is intended to be a free-standing result. We actually only need the slightly easier case where
B
is defined by a bilinear (as opposed to bi-affine) function and
\phi
is additive in each variable separately, but the more general statement is of some interest, so we give it here.

As a preparatory lemma, we shall show that
\phi
is not only affine in each variable separately, but it also respects all 4-arrangements, which is equivalent to saying that the vertical derivative
\phi^{\prime}
is a Freiman homomorphism in each variable separately. We have already noted that this is very easy to show when
\phi
is linear in each variable separately, but it becomes slightly trickier in the affine case, since then we do not know that every pair of cross-sections of
B
has a non-empty intersection.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
G=\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}
and let
H
be a finite Abelian group. Let
B
be a bilinear Bohr set of codimension
k
and rank
t
with
t>6k
. Let
\phi:B\to H
be a Freiman homomorphism in each variable separately, and let
\phi^{\prime}
be the vertical derivative of
\phi
. Then
\phi^{\prime}
is a Freiman homomorphism in each variable separately.

Proof.

Recall that
B^{\prime}
is the set of all pairs
(x,h)
such that there exists
y
with
(x,y)
and
(x,y+h)
in
B
. Also, the vertical derivative
\phi^{\prime}(x,h)
is defined to be
\phi(x,y+h)-\phi(x,y)
whenever
(x,h)\in B^{\prime}
.

It is trivial that
\phi^{\prime}
is a Freiman homomorphism on each column, since each
\phi_{x\bullet}
is affine on
B_{x\bullet}
and therefore
\phi^{\prime}_{x\bullet}
is even linear on
B^{\prime}_{x\bullet}
.

Now let us pick an arbitrary
(x,h)\in B^{\prime}
. As we noted at the end of the previous section, since
B^{\prime}_{\bullet h}
is non-empty, it has density at least
p^{-k}
in
G
. Also,
B_{x\bullet}
has density at least
p^{-k}
in
G
.

By Lemma
5.3
, if we choose
x_{1}
uniformly at random from
B^{\prime}_{\bullet h}
, the probability that the restriction of
\beta_{x_{1}\bullet}
to
B_{x\bullet}
is a surjection is at least
1-p^{3k-t}
, and if it is a surjection, then
B_{x_{1}\bullet}\cap B_{x\bullet}
has density
p^{-k}
in
B_{x\bullet}
and therefore
p^{-2k}
in
G
. If we then pick
x_{2}
uniformly at random from
B^{\prime}_{\bullet h}
, the same argument gives us that
B_{x_{1}\bullet}\cap B_{x_{2}\bullet}\cap B_{x\bullet}
has density
p^{-3k}
with probability at least
1-p^{4k-t}
. And if we now choose
x_{3}
such that
x_{1}-x_{2}=x_{3}-x
, we have that
B_{x_{1}\bullet}\cap B_{x_{2}\bullet}\cap B_{x\bullet}\subset B_{x_{3}\bullet}
, so
B_{x_{1}\bullet}\cap B_{x_{2}\bullet}\cap B_{x_{3}\bullet}\cap B_{x\bullet}
has density
p^{-3k}
.

In particular, it is non-empty, so we can pick
y
that belongs to it. Then
y+h
belongs to it as well, since the
x_{i}
and
x
all belong to
B^{\prime}_{\bullet h}
. But

\phi(x_{1},y)-\phi(x_{2},y)=\phi(x_{3},y)-\phi(x,y)

and

\phi(x_{1},y+h)-\phi(x_{2},y+h)=\phi(x_{3},y+h)-\phi(x,y+h),

which implies that

\phi^{\prime}(x_{1},h)-\phi^{\prime}(x_{2},h)=\phi^{\prime}(x_{3},h)-\phi^{\prime}(x,h).

We have proved that for each
(x,h)\in B^{\prime}
, out of all triples
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in B^{\prime}_{\bullet h}
such that
x_{1}-x_{2}=x_{3}-x
, the proportion such that

\phi^{\prime}(x_{1},h)-\phi^{\prime}(x_{2},h)=\phi^{\prime}(x_{3},h)-\phi^{\prime}(x,h)

is at least
1-p^{4k-t}
. Note that this is a stronger statement than saying that the proportion of additive quadruples in
B_{\bullet h}
that are respected by
\phi^{\prime}_{\bullet h}
is at least
1-p^{4k-t}
, since the above statement applies to every
x
, and not just almost every
x
.

Thus, for every
x\in B^{\prime}_{\bullet h}
,
\phi^{\prime}(x,h)
is the most popular value of
\phi^{\prime}(x_{2},h)+\phi^{\prime}(x_{3},h)-\phi^{\prime}(x_{1},h)
. Since we also know, by Lemma
6.6
, that
\phi^{\prime}_{\bullet h}
agrees with an affine map on a subset of
B^{\prime}_{\bullet h}
of density at least
1-5p^{4k-t}
, it follows that
\phi^{\prime}
is equal to that affine map.

This proves that
\phi^{\prime}_{\bullet h}
is an affine map on
B^{\prime}_{\bullet h}
whenever
B^{\prime}_{\bullet h}
is non-empty, which completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

We need a second preparatory lemma as well.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
G=\mathbb{F}_{p}^{n}
and let
B\subset G^{2}
be a bilinear Bohr set of codimension
k
and rank
t
with
t>3k
. Then
B
, when considered as a bipartite graph, consists of one connected component together with isolated vertices. Moreover, the density of the set of isolated vertices in each vertex set is at most
p^{k-t}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.4
the density of columns
B_{x\bullet}
that fail to have density
p^{-k}
is at most
p^{k-t}
, and similarly for rows. In bipartite-graph terms, that says that except in a subset of density at most
p^{k-t}
on both sides, all vertices have neighbourhoods of density
p^{-k}
, and in particular are not isolated.

Let
x
and
y
be such that
B_{x\bullet}
and
B_{\bullet y}
are non-empty, and hence have density at least
p^{-k}
. Then by Lemma
5.3
, if
u
is chosen randomly from
B_{\bullet y}
, the probability that the restriction of
\beta_{u\bullet}
to
B_{x\bullet}
is a surjection is at least
1-p^{3k-t}>0
, and if it is a surjection then there exists
v\in B_{x\bullet}
such that
\beta(u,v)=z
. This gives us an edge linking
B_{\bullet y}
to
B_{x\bullet}
, and therefore a path of length 3 from
x
to
y
.

Thus, every non-isolated vertex one one side is connected to every non-isolated vertex on the other side, from which the result follows easily.
∎

Now let us move on to the main theorem. Our approach is as follows. Let
B=\{(x,y)\in G^{2}:\beta(x,y)=z\}
, where
\beta:G^{2}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}^{k}
is a bi-affine map of codimension
k
and rank
t
. Then by Lemma
5.3
, if we choose
x
randomly, the probability that the linear map
\beta_{x\bullet}
is of full rank is at least
1-p^{k-t}
. Let
x_{1}
be an element of
G
with this property.

We can extend
\phi
from
B
to
B\cup G_{x_{1}\bullet}
by extending
\phi_{x_{1}\bullet}
arbitrarily from
B_{x_{1}\bullet}
to an affine map on the whole of
G_{x_{1}\bullet}
. If we do that, the resulting extension is still affine in each variable separately. This is obvious for the columns. As for the rows, it follows since an affine map on an affine subspace can be extended arbitrarily to a function defined on the union of that subspace and an extra point, and it will remain affine.

Our aim now is to prove that there is a unique further extension to a bi-affine function defined on all of
G^{2}
. To do that we shall being by saying what the function is almost everywhere. Then we shall prove that it is well-defined and a Freiman homomorphism in each variable separately. Once that is done, Lemma
6.24
(in fact, an easy special case of that lemma) will remove the “almost” and give us a bi-affine function defined on the whole of
G^{2}
.

We know that the set of
x
such that the map
y\mapsto(\beta(x_{1},y),\beta(x,y))
has full rank has density at least
1-\delta
. Let this set be
U
. For any
x\in U
and any
y\in G
, we define
\psi(x,y)
as follows. Let
\beta(x,y)=u
. We begin by finding
h
such that
\beta(x,y-h)=z
and
\beta^{\prime}(x_{1},h)=u-z
, where
\beta^{\prime}
is, as usual, the vertical derivative of
\beta
. This we can do because the map
h\mapsto(\beta^{\prime}(x_{1},h),\beta(x,y-h))
also has full rank. Next, we find
w
such that
\beta_{(x_{1}+w)\bullet}
and
\beta_{(x-w)\bullet}
have full rank, and also such that
\beta^{\prime}(x_{1}+w,h)=0
. Since
\beta^{\prime}(x_{1},h)=\beta^{\prime}(x,h)=u-z
and
\beta^{\prime}
is bi-affine, it follows that
\beta^{\prime}(x-w,h)=0
.

We now pick
y_{1}
arbitrarily, and
y_{2},y_{3}
such that
\beta(x_{1}+w,y_{2})=\beta(x-w,y_{3})=z
. This gives us a 4-arrangement whose points, in the usual order, are
(x_{1},y_{1})
,
(x_{1},y_{1}+h)
,
(x_{1}+w,y_{2})
,
(x_{1}+w,y_{2}+h)
,
(x-w,y_{3})
,
(x-w,y_{3}+h)
,
(x,y-h)
, and
(x,y)
.

We know the values of
\phi
at all these points except the last. That is because the first two points have first coordinate
x_{1}
, and we have extended
\phi
so that it is defined on all such points. As for the remaining points apart from
(x,y)
, they have been chosen to belong to
B
. For instance,
(x_{2},y_{3}+h)
belongs to
B
because
\beta(x-w,y_{3}+h)=\beta(x-w,y_{3})+\beta^{\prime}(x-w,h)=z+0=z
. So we now choose
\psi
in the only way possible so that it will respect this 4-arrangement. That is,

\displaystyle\psi(x,y)=\phi(x_{1},y_{1})

\displaystyle-\phi(x_{1},y_{1}+h)-\phi(x_{1}+w,y_{2})

\displaystyle+\phi(x_{1}+w,y_{2}+h)-\phi(x-w,y_{3})+\phi(x-w,y_{3}+h)+\phi(x,y-h).

We now prove that
\psi
is well-defined on
U\times G
.

Lemma 7.3
.

The value of the right-hand side in the formula just given for
\psi
does not depend on our choices of
w,h,y_{1},y_{2}
and
y_{3}
.

Proof.

Since
\phi
is affine in the columns, it has a well-defined vertical derivative
\phi^{\prime}
, which allows us to rewrite the formula as

\psi(x,y)=-\phi^{\prime}(x_{1},h)+\phi^{\prime}(x_{1}+w,h)+\phi^{\prime}(x-w,h)+\phi(x,y-h),

which is independent of
y_{1},y_{2}
and
y_{3}
.

Note also that if we replace
h
by
h^{\prime}
, then the difference to the right-hand side is

-\phi^{\prime}(x_{1},h^{\prime}-h)+\phi^{\prime}(x_{1}+w,h^{\prime}-h)+\phi^{\prime}(x-w,h^{\prime}-h)-\phi^{\prime}(x,h^{\prime}-h),

which is well-defined, since each column of
B^{\prime}
is a linear subspace and not just an affine subspace.

By Lemma
7.1
,
\phi^{\prime}
is affine in the rows, so this difference is zero.

Now suppose that we replace
w
by
w^{\prime}
. Then the difference to the right-hand side is

\phi^{\prime}(x_{1}+w^{\prime},h)-\phi^{\prime}(x_{1}+w,h)+\phi^{\prime}(x-w^{\prime},h)-\phi^{\prime}(x-w,h).

Since
\phi^{\prime}
is affine in the rows, and
(x_{1}+w^{\prime})-(x_{1}+w)+(x-w^{\prime})-(x-w)=0
, this difference is also zero.

That does not complete the proof, because a change to both
h
and
w
is not necessarily decomposable into a change to
h
followed by a change to
w
. We deal with this by using Lemma
7.2
, which will provide us with a path from one choice of
(w,h)
to another that changes only one variable at a time.

Note that
(w,h)
is a possible pair if
\beta^{\prime}(x_{1},h)=u-z
,
\beta(x,y-h)=z
, and
\beta^{\prime}(x_{1}+w,h)=\beta^{\prime}(x-w,h)=0
. Because the map
y\mapsto(\beta(x_{1},y),\beta(x,y))
has full rank, the first two conditions tell us that
h
lies in an affine subspace
V\subset G
of codimension
2k
. Also, if they hold, then, as we have already commented, the condition
\beta^{\prime}(x_{1}+w,h)
implies the condition
\beta^{\prime}(x-w,h)=0
.

Therefore, the set of all possible
(w,h)
is the intersection of
G\times V
with the bilinear Bohr set
\{(w,h):\beta^{\prime}(x_{1}+w,h)=0\}
, which has rank
t
in
G^{2}
and therefore rank at least
t-k
in
G\times V
. Therefore, by Lemma
7.2
, if
(w_{1},h_{1})
and
(w_{2},h_{2})
are possible values for
(w,h)
we can find
w^{\prime}
and
h^{\prime}
such that
(w_{1},h^{\prime})
,
(w^{\prime},h^{\prime})
and
(w^{\prime},h_{2})
are possible values. This, together with the fact that changing just one variable does not alter the value of the formula for
\psi(x,y)
, completes the proof that
\psi
is well-defined on
U\times G
.
∎

The next step is to prove that
\psi
is a Freiman homomorphism in each row and column.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
\psi
be defined as above and assume that
t>6k
. Then
\psi
is affine in each column.

Proof.

Let
x\in U
and let
y_{1}-y_{2}=y_{3}-y_{4}
. For
i=1,2,3
let
h_{i}
be such that
\beta(x,y_{i}-h_{i})=z
and
\beta^{\prime}(x_{1},h_{i})=\beta(x,y_{i})-z
. Now let
h_{4}
be such that
h_{1}-h_{2}=h_{3}-h_{4}
. Since
\beta
and
\beta^{\prime}
are bi-affine, we have that
\beta(x,y_{4}-h_{4})=z
and
\beta^{\prime}(x_{1},h_{4})=\beta(x,y_{4})-z
.

Note that there are several ways of choosing
h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}
above, and that for each
i
, the constraint on
h_{i}
is that it should lie in a certain affine subspace of codimension
k
. From Lemma
5.4
, if we choose
h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}
randomly from these affine subspaces, then the probability that the equations
\beta^{\prime}(x_{1}+w,h_{1})=\beta^{\prime}(x_{1}+w,h_{2})=\beta^{\prime}(x_{1}+w,h_{3})=0
are satisfied by all
w
in a set of density
p^{-3k}
is at least
1-p^{6k-t}
. (The lemma gives a bound of
1-p^{3k-t}
, and we then condition on an event of probability
p^{-3k}
.)

If those equations are satisfied, then so is the equation
\beta^{\prime}(x_{1}+w,h_{4})=0
